# O&G Notes — Section 2: Anatomy

*Written as prose and tables — deliberately image-independent. Aligned to RANZCOG and the ACSQHC Third and Fourth Degree Perineal Tears Clinical Care Standard for tear classification; SA Health PPG for repair detail. Scope: what is examinable in a written paper — relations, supply, drainage, diameters and the clinical consequences of each.*

## 2.1 The bony pelvis

The **true pelvis** (below the pelvic brim) is the birth canal. Its three planes have different shapes, and this is the whole explanation for the mechanism of labour.

| Plane | AP diameter | Transverse diameter | Widest axis |
|---|---|---|---|
| Inlet (brim) | true conjugate ~11 cm; **obstetric conjugate ~10.5 cm** (sacral promontory → nearest/posterior point of symphysis — the true limiting diameter); diagonal conjugate ~12.5 cm (**the only one clinically measurable**, promontory → inferior symphysis) | ~13 cm | **Transverse** |
| Mid-cavity | ~12 cm | ~12 cm (interspinous ~10.5 cm — **narrowest pelvic diameter overall**) | round |
| Outlet | ~12.5–13 cm | intertuberous ~11 cm | **AP** |

**Memory hook:** widest **transverse at the inlet**, widest **AP at the outlet**. The fetal head therefore **engages in the transverse, then rotates to occipito-anterior** to deliver — the rotation exists because the pelvis changes shape, not because the head "prefers" it.

The **ischial spines** are the reference point for everything: station 0 by definition, the narrowest interspinous diameter, the landmark for pudendal block, and the attachment of the sacrospinous ligament (used in sacrospinous fixation for vault prolapse).

| Pelvis type | Inlet shape | Obstetric relevance |
|---|---|---|
| **Gynaecoid** (~50%) | round/slightly oval transversely | most favourable |
| Android | heart-shaped, narrow forepelvis, prominent spines | deep transverse arrest, OP position, arrest of descent |
| Anthropoid | oval AP > transverse | favours **direct OA or OP** delivery |
| Platypelloid | flat, wide transverse, short AP | head engages transverse, **fails to rotate** |

**The short version**
- Obstetric conjugate is the true limiting inlet diameter; diagonal conjugate is the one your finger can reach.
- Interspinous distance is the narrowest diameter in the entire pelvis.
- Inlet transverse → outlet AP explains internal rotation.

## 2.2 The pelvic floor

The **pelvic diaphragm** = **levator ani** (pubococcygeus, puborectalis, iliococcygeus) + **coccygeus**, slung from the tendinous arch of the obturator fascia. Innervation: direct branches from **S3–S5 (nerve to levator ani)** with a variable contribution from the pudendal nerve.

**Puborectalis** forms the sling around the anorectal junction and maintains the anorectal angle — the principal mechanism of faecal continence, more so than the external anal sphincter at rest. Avulsion of the puborectalis from the pubic ramus occurs in ~10–15% of first vaginal births (higher with forceps) and is a major driver of later prolapse.

Below the levators, the **perineal membrane** separates the **deep perineal pouch** (external urethral sphincter, compressor urethrae, deep transverse perineal muscle) from the **superficial perineal pouch** (bulbospongiosus, ischiocavernosus, superficial transverse perineal muscle, vestibular bulbs, **Bartholin's [greater vestibular] glands** — which drain at 4 and 8 o'clock in the vestibule, hence the classic posterolateral Bartholin's cyst).

The **perineal body** is the fibromuscular central tendon between vagina and anus, into which bulbospongiosus, superficial and deep transverse perineal muscles, the external anal sphincter and the levators all insert. **It is the structure destroyed in a second-degree tear or episiotomy, and its integrity underpins both posterior compartment support and anal continence.**

**Uterine support** — the discriminator that matters:

| Ligament | Composition | Does it actually support? |
|---|---|---|
| **Cardinal (transverse cervical, Mackenrodt's)** | fibromuscular, cervix/upper vagina → lateral pelvic wall; **carries the uterine vessels** | **Yes — principal lateral support** |
| **Uterosacral** | cervix → sacrum | **Yes — principal posterior support**; the structure plicated in prolapse surgery |
| Pubocervical | cervix → pubis | Yes, anterior (weak) |
| Round | cornu → inguinal canal → labium majus (contains artery of Sampson) | **No** — maintains anteversion only; source of round ligament pain |
| Broad | peritoneal fold | **No** — a mesentery, not a ligament |
| Suspensory (infundibulopelvic) | ovary → pelvic sidewall; **contains ovarian vessels** | supports ovary; **the pedicle clamped in oophorectomy — the ureter runs close beneath it** |
| Ovarian ligament | ovary → cornu (gubernaculum remnant) | no |

## 2.3 The genital tract

Uterus: fundus, body, isthmus (becomes the lower segment in pregnancy — the site of LSCS incision), cervix. Layers: endometrium (functionalis shed, basalis regenerates), myometrium, perimetrium. Normally **anteverted and anteflexed** (~80%); retroversion is a normal variant but relevant to incarceration of a retroverted gravid uterus at 12–16 weeks.

Cervix: endocervix lined by columnar epithelium, ectocervix by stratified squamous. The **squamocolumnar junction** everts onto the ectocervix under oestrogen (puberty, pregnancy, COCP) and the exposed columnar epithelium undergoes squamous metaplasia — the **transformation zone**. This is where HPV-driven dysplasia arises and therefore what a cervical screening sample and colposcopy must sample. It regresses into the canal after menopause, which is why colposcopy is more often unsatisfactory in older women.

Fallopian tube: interstitial (intramural) → isthmus → **ampulla** (fertilisation site; **commonest site of ectopic, ~70%**) → infundibulum/fimbriae. **Interstitial (cornual) ectopics present later (~8–12 weeks) and bleed catastrophically** because of the surrounding myometrial vascularity.

Vagina: stratified squamous, no glands; lubricated by transudate and cervical mucus. Upper third from Müllerian duct, lower part from urogenital sinus — the embryological seam that explains the **watershed at the hymen** in both lymphatic drainage and nerve supply.

**Key surgical relation — "water under the bridge":** the **ureter passes beneath the uterine artery** approximately **1.5–2 cm lateral to the cervix at the level of the internal os**, within the cardinal ligament. This is the site of ureteric injury at hysterectomy and at uterine artery ligation for PPH. The ureter is also at risk at the pelvic brim (crossing the bifurcation of the common iliac) and where it runs beneath the infundibulopelvic ligament.

## 2.4 Blood supply

| Structure | Arterial supply | Origin |
|---|---|---|
| Ovary (and lateral tube) | ovarian artery | **abdominal aorta, ~L2** (below the renal arteries) |
| Uterus, medial tube, upper vagina | uterine artery | **anterior division of internal iliac** |
| Vagina (mid) | vaginal artery | internal iliac (or uterine) |
| Vulva, perineum, lower vagina | internal pudendal artery | anterior division of internal iliac |
| Lower vagina/perineum (accessory) | inferior rectal, posterior labial | internal pudendal |

The uterine and ovarian arteries **anastomose freely within the broad ligament** — which is why unilateral internal iliac ligation rarely produces uterine necrosis, and why uterine artery embolisation for fibroids can still fail via ovarian collaterals.

Venous drainage is the classic asymmetry: **right ovarian vein → IVC; left ovarian vein → left renal vein.** This underlies the left-sided predominance of pelvic congestion syndrome and the right-sided predominance of postpartum ovarian vein thrombosis (right vein is longer, crosses the ureter, and drains directly to IVC).

## 2.5 Lymphatic drainage

Lymphatics follow arteries, so **ovarian drainage is high (para-aortic), not pelvic** — which is why ovarian cancer staging requires para-aortic nodal assessment and why an ovarian cancer can have para-aortic nodes with clean pelvic nodes.

| Structure | Primary nodes |
|---|---|
| **Ovary, fallopian tube (lateral)** | **para-aortic** (follow ovarian vessels to L2) |
| Uterine fundus | para-aortic, plus a minor route **along the round ligament to superficial inguinal** |
| Uterine body | external iliac |
| **Cervix** | **obturator, internal and external iliac, then common iliac and para-aortic**; sacral |
| Upper ⅔ vagina | internal and external iliac |
| **Lower ⅓ vagina (below hymen), vulva, perineum** | **superficial inguinal → deep inguinal (node of Cloquet) → external iliac** |

**Clue → answer:** an inguinal node metastasis from a gynaecological primary means the tumour is **at or below the hymen** — vulva, lower vagina, or (rarely, via the round ligament) fundal.

## 2.6 Nerve supply and the anatomy of labour analgesia

| Structure | Segmental supply | Pain in labour |
|---|---|---|
| Uterine body, cervix (stretch) | **T10–L1** sympathetic via inferior hypogastric plexus | **first stage** — visceral, poorly localised, referred to back/lower abdomen |
| Cervix, upper vagina | S2–S4 parasympathetic (pelvic splanchnic) | |
| Lower vagina, vulva, perineum | **pudendal nerve, S2–S4** | **second stage** — somatic, sharp, well localised |
| Anterior vulva/mons | ilioinguinal, genitofemoral (L1) | not covered by pudendal block |

Consequences: an **epidural must cover T10–L1 for the first stage and extend to S2–S4 for the second stage**; a **pudendal block (needle guided to the ischial spine, injecting medial and inferior to it, targeting the nerve as it re-enters through the lesser sciatic foramen) covers only the perineum** — adequate for outlet forceps or perineal repair, useless for uterine pain, and it will **miss the anterior vulva** (L1 territory), which needs local infiltration.

## 2.7 The fetal skull

Bones: 2 frontal, 2 parietal, 2 temporal, 1 occipital. Sutures: sagittal, coronal, lambdoid, frontal (metopic). Fontanelles: **anterior (bregma)** — diamond-shaped, 4 sutures, closes 18–24 months; **posterior (lambda)** — triangular, 3 sutures, closes ~6–8 weeks. **On vaginal examination, feeling a triangular fontanelle in the anterior pelvis = occiput anterior; feeling a diamond fontanelle anteriorly = occiput posterior or deflexed.**

Landmarks: occiput, vertex (between fontanelles, bounded by parietal eminences), sinciput/brow, bregma, mentum (chin), glabella.

| Diameter | Measurement | Attitude / presentation |
|---|---|---|
| **Suboccipito-bregmatic (SOB)** | **9.5 cm** | **fully flexed vertex — the optimal diameter** |
| Suboccipito-frontal | 10.0–10.5 cm | partially deflexed vertex |
| Occipito-frontal (OF) | **11.5 cm** | deflexed / "military" — the OP diameter |
| **Mento-vertical (MV)** | **13.5 cm** | **brow — largest, will not deliver vaginally at term** |
| Submento-vertical | 11.5 cm | partially extended face |
| **Submento-bregmatic (SMB)** | **9.5 cm** | **fully extended face — mento-anterior can deliver vaginally** |
| Biparietal (BPD) | 9.5 cm | transverse, all vertex presentations |
| Bitemporal | 8.0–8.2 cm | transverse, smallest |

**Memory hook:** the head presents its smallest diameter when it is either **fully flexed (SOB 9.5)** or **fully extended (SMB 9.5)**. Everything in between is worse, and the halfway point — **brow, MV 13.5 cm — is the worst possible attitude**. Hence: **face mento-anterior may deliver vaginally; face mento-posterior and brow may not** (mento-posterior requires the neck to extend further than anatomically possible).

**Moulding** (overlapping at sutures) and **caput succedaneum** (diffuse subcutaneous oedema, **crosses suture lines**, present at birth, resolves in days) are physiological. Discriminate from **cephalhaematoma** (subperiosteal, **does not cross sutures**, appears hours after birth, jaundice risk) and **subgaleal haemorrhage** (boggy, crosses sutures, shifts with position, **can exsanguinate — associated with ventouse**).

## 2.8 The perineum and classification of tears

The perineum is the diamond between the pubic symphysis, ischial tuberosities and coccyx, divided by an intertuberous line into the **urogenital triangle** (anterior) and **anal triangle** (posterior, containing the ischiorectal fossae and the pudendal canal of Alcock on the lateral wall of each).

**Classification (ACSQHC Clinical Care Standard / RANZCOG, adopting the RCOG–Sultan system):**

| Degree | Definition |
|---|---|
| **First** | perineal or vaginal **skin/mucosa only** |
| **Second** | into the **perineal muscles**, anal sphincter intact (**an episiotomy is by definition a second-degree injury**) |
| **3a** | **<50%** of external anal sphincter (EAS) thickness torn |
| **3b** | **>50%** of EAS thickness torn |
| **3c** | **EAS and internal anal sphincter (IAS)** both torn |
| **Fourth** | EAS + IAS + **anal epithelium** breached |

⚑ Older texts describe "third degree = any sphincter involvement" without subclassification; **the 3a/3b/3c split is now standard and must be documented**, because it determines repair technique and prognosis. Third- and fourth-degree tears together are termed **OASIS** (obstetric anal sphincter injuries).

**Every woman after vaginal birth requires a systematic perineal and digital rectal examination before repair** — the standard exists because 3a and IAS injuries are otherwise missed, and missed OASIS is the leading preventable cause of later faecal incontinence.

Risk factors: **first vaginal birth, instrumental delivery (forceps > ventouse), midline episiotomy, birthweight >4 kg, prolonged second stage, OP position, shoulder dystocia, induction, epidural.** A **mediolateral episiotomy cut at 60° from the midline at crowning** reduces OASIS risk; **midline episiotomy increases it** by directing extension into the sphincter.

Repair principles: theatre, regional or general anaesthesia, good lighting and assistance. **IAS — end-to-end with interrupted 3-0 PDS; EAS — overlapping repair preferred for full-thickness (3b/3c/4th), end-to-end mandatory for 3a (there is insufficient torn tissue to overlap), using 2-0 PDS with buried knots.** Post-repair: broad-spectrum antibiotics (IV cephazolin + metronidazole, then oral amoxicillin–clavulanate for 5 days; clindamycin ± gentamicin if penicillin-anaphylactic), **laxatives and bulking agents for ~2 weeks** (constipation disrupts the repair), physiotherapy, and follow-up with consideration of endoanal ultrasound and manometry before a subsequent birth.

## Rapid-Recall Discriminators

| Clue / question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Narrowest diameter in the whole pelvis | **Interspinous (~10.5 cm)** |
| Only inlet conjugate you can measure clinically | **Diagonal conjugate (~12.5 cm)**; obstetric conjugate (~10.5) is the true limit |
| Why the head engages transverse and delivers OA | **Inlet is widest transverse, outlet is widest AP** |
| Ovarian cancer with para-aortic nodes and clean pelvic nodes | **Expected** — ovary drains to para-aortic via ovarian vessels |
| Inguinal node metastasis from a gynae primary | Tumour is **at or below the hymen** (vulva/lower vagina) |
| Ureter injured at hysterectomy — where? | **~1.5–2 cm lateral to cervix at internal os, under the uterine artery** ("water under the bridge") |
| Structure clamped in oophorectomy, with ureter beneath | **Infundibulopelvic (suspensory) ligament** |
| Ligament providing principal uterine support | **Cardinal** (lateral) and **uterosacral** (posterior); **round and broad do NOT support** |
| Diamond fontanelle felt anteriorly on VE | **Deflexed / occipito-posterior** (triangular anteriorly = OA) |
| Smallest presenting diameter | **SOB 9.5 cm** (flexed vertex) — equalled by SMB 9.5 cm (full face) |
| Largest, undeliverable diameter | **Mento-vertical 13.5 cm — brow presentation** |
| Face presentation that can deliver vaginally | **Mento-anterior** only |
| Swelling crossing suture lines, present at birth | **Caput** (cephalhaematoma does not cross; subgaleal crosses and can exsanguinate) |
| Pudendal block landmark, and what it misses | **Ischial spine**; misses the **anterior vulva (L1 — ilioinguinal/genitofemoral)** and all uterine pain |
| 40% of EAS torn | **3a — end-to-end repair** (not enough tissue to overlap) |
| EAS + IAS torn, anal mucosa intact | **3c** (4th degree requires breach of anal epithelium) |
| Episiotomy angle and type to reduce OASIS | **Mediolateral, 60° from midline at crowning**; midline increases OASIS |

### Sources
- Australian Commission on Safety and Quality in Health Care, *Third and Fourth Degree Perineal Tears Clinical Care Standard* (2021).
- SA Health, *Third and Fourth Degree Tear Management* Perinatal Practice Guideline (classification, suture material, antibiotics, laxatives).
- RANZCOG (adopting RCOG Green-top 29), *The Management of Third- and Fourth-Degree Perineal Tears*.
- Standard anatomical reference for pelvic diameters, fetal skull diameters and lymphatic drainage (Jaypee *Pelvis and Fetal Skull*; StatPearls *Delivery, Face and Brow Presentation*).

---

# O&G Notes — Section 3: Antenatal Care and Screening

*Australian practice. Anchored to the Australian Pregnancy Care Guidelines, RANZCOG statements, RACGP Red Book (first antenatal visit), National Blood Authority anti-D guideline (2024), Australian Immunisation Handbook, ADIPS 2025 consensus and Queensland Clinical Guidelines. Where Australian practice differs from UK/NICE, this is stated. ⚑ marks a change from older textbooks.*

## 3.1 The booking visit

Ideally **within the first 10 weeks**. Purpose is risk stratification, dating, screening and shared decision-making about model of care.

**Booking bloods (routine):** FBC; **blood group and antibody screen**; rubella IgG; syphilis serology; HBsAg; **hepatitis C antibody**; HIV. **Ferritin** is increasingly routine (risk-based in older guidance, universal in many Australian services). Risk-based additions: haemoglobin electrophoresis/HPLC (ethnicity, low MCV/MCH, family history), TSH (thyroid disease, T1DM, symptoms — **not universal**), vitamin D (dark skin, veiling, low sun exposure, BMI >30).

**Other booking tests:** **urine MCS for asymptomatic bacteriuria** (culture, not dipstick — dipstick is insufficiently sensitive); **chlamydia NAAT if aged <30 or with risk factors** (⚑ this is an Australian point — the UK does not routinely screen for chlamydia in pregnancy); cervical screening test if due (safe in pregnancy).

**Clinical assessment:** height, weight, BMI; BP; **EPDS depression screen (and repeat at least once later in pregnancy)**; domestic/family violence enquiry; smoking, alcohol and other drug use; medication review for teratogens; **pre-eclampsia risk assessment**; **GDM risk assessment**.

Acting on the risk assessment: high pre-eclampsia risk → **low-dose aspirin 100–150 mg nocte, commenced between 12 and 16 weeks** and continued to ~36 weeks, plus calcium supplementation if dietary intake is low.

**Dating.** Use LMP only if cycles are regular and dates certain. Otherwise, or if there is discrepancy: **CRL is the most accurate parameter between 7 and 13+6 weeks (±5 days)**; after 14 weeks use HC/BPD/FL (±7–10 days, degrading with gestation). ⚑ **Ultrasound dating overrides LMP if the discrepancy exceeds ~5–7 days in the first trimester.** Never re-date on a third-trimester scan — a small fetus late in pregnancy is FGR until proven otherwise, not a wrong date.

**The short version**
- Booking by 10 weeks; dating by CRL if <14 weeks.
- Urine **culture** for asymptomatic bacteriuria — untreated it causes pyelonephritis and preterm birth.
- Aspirin decision is made at booking and started 12–16 weeks.

## 3.2 Visit schedule

RANZCOG accepts **~10 visits for an uncomplicated nulliparous woman and ~7 for a multiparous woman**; exact gestations vary between states and models of care.

| Gestation | Content |
|---|---|
| **<10 weeks (booking)** | history, exam, booking bloods, urine MCS, EPDS, risk assessment, dating scan if needed |
| 11+0–13+6 | **cFTS** (NT + serum) if chosen; NIPT can be done from 10 weeks |
| 16 | review screening results, BP, urine; commence aspirin by now if indicated |
| **18–22 (usually 19–20)** | **morphology scan** ± cervical length, placental site |
| 24 | BP, urine, **SFH from 24 weeks onwards** |
| **24–28** | **75 g OGTT for GDM** |
| **28** | FBC (± ferritin), repeat antibody screen, **anti-D dose 1 (625 IU)** |
| 31 (nullip) | BP, urine, SFH |
| **34** | **anti-D dose 2 (625 IU)** |
| **35–37** | **GBS swab** (if universal screening used); repeat depression screen |
| 36 | **confirm presentation** (palpation ± USS); discuss ECV if breech |
| 38, 40, 41 | BP/urine/SFH; **discuss IOL at 41 weeks**, membrane sweep |

BP, urinalysis and SFH at every visit from 24 weeks; fetal heart auscultation from ~24 weeks (of reassurance value only, not diagnostic).

## 3.3 RhD status and anti-D prophylaxis

Alloimmunisation occurs when an RhD-negative woman is exposed to RhD-positive fetal cells; the resulting IgG anti-D crosses the placenta in *subsequent* pregnancies and causes haemolytic disease of the fetus and newborn.

**Australian regimen (National Blood Authority, 2024):**

| Situation | Dose |
|---|---|
| **Routine antenatal prophylaxis** | **625 IU IM at 28 weeks AND 625 IU IM at 34 weeks** |
| **Sensitising event ≤12+6 weeks** | **250 IU IM** |
| **Sensitising event ≥13+0 weeks** | **625 IU IM** |
| **Postnatal (baby confirmed RhD positive)** | **625 IU IM** |
| Large FMH ≥6 mL fetal red cells | 625 IU **plus 100 IU per additional mL** of fetal red cells beyond 6 mL |

Timing: **give within 72 hours**; if missed, still give up to **10 days** (partial protection). Routine doses may be given within ~2 weeks of the scheduled gestation if logistics require.

**Sensitising events:** miscarriage or threatened miscarriage (with heavy/repeated bleeding or pain), termination, ectopic, molar pregnancy, CVS/amniocentesis/fetal blood sampling, **abdominal trauma**, **antepartum haemorrhage**, **external cephalic version**, intrauterine death, and delivery itself.

**Postnatal FMH quantitation (Kleihauer or flow cytometry) is required for every RhD-negative woman delivering an RhD-positive baby**, to identify the large bleeds needing supplementary dosing. Repeat testing 48 h after IV or 72 h after IM administration when a large FMH has been treated.

⚑ **RhD NIPT (cell-free fetal DNA fetal blood group genotyping)** is now available in Australia through Lifeblood **from 15 weeks**, **MBS-funded/bulk-billed** for non-alloimmunised RhD-negative women (not validated in multiple pregnancy). **If the fetus is predicted RhD negative, antenatal anti-D is not indicated.** This is a genuine change from the universal-prophylaxis model in older texts.

⚑ **Anti-D does not treat established alloimmunisation.** If the antibody screen already shows anti-D, giving more is pointless — refer to MFM for antibody titres/quantitation and **MCA peak systolic velocity Dopplers (>1.5 MoM = significant fetal anaemia → consider intrauterine transfusion)**.

**Australia vs UK:** Australia uses **two doses of 625 IU at 28 and 34 weeks**. The UK (NICE) offers either **a single 1500 IU dose at 28 weeks** or **two 500 IU doses at 28 and 34 weeks**. Quoting the UK figures in an Australian paper is a marked error.

## 3.4 Aneuploidy and structural screening

Screening is **opt-in after counselling**, not automatic. All screening results are risks, not diagnoses.

| Test | Timing | Components | Performance for T21 |
|---|---|---|---|
| **cFTS** | **NT at 11+0–13+6 (CRL 45–84 mm)**; serum from 9–13+6 | NT + free β-hCG + PAPP-A + maternal age | DR **~85–90%**, FPR ~5% |
| **NIPT (cfDNA)** | **from 10 weeks** | placental cfDNA fraction | DR **>99%**, FPR **<0.1%** |
| Second trimester serum (quad) | 15–20 weeks | AFP, hCG, uE3, inhibin A | DR ~75–80% |
| **Morphology scan** | **18–22 weeks** (commonly 19–20 in Australia) | structural survey, placental site, ± cervical length | structural anomalies |

**The short version**
- **NIPT is a screening test, not diagnostic** — every high-risk result needs confirmation by CVS or amniocentesis. Its **PPV depends on prior risk**: in a 25-year-old, PPV for T21 may be ~50–70% and for rare trisomies/microdeletions considerably lower.
- ⚑ **NIPT is not MBS-funded in Australia** (typically $400–500 out of pocket), unlike the separately funded RhD NIPT. cFTS is partly rebatable. This cost difference drives real inequity in access and is worth stating in a short-answer response.
- **Failed/"no-call" NIPT (1–5%)** is usually **low fetal fraction**: high maternal BMI, too-early gestation, twin/vanishing twin, some anticoagulants, and — importantly — **aneuploid placentas (T13, T18, triploidy)**. A no-call is itself a mild risk marker; repeat or offer diagnostic testing rather than reassure.

**Diagnostic testing:** **CVS from 11–14 weeks** (never before 10 weeks — earlier sampling is linked to limb-reduction defects), **amniocentesis from 15 weeks**. ⚑ **Procedure-related miscarriage risk in contemporary series is ~0.1–0.2% above background, not the 1% (amniocentesis) or 1–2% (CVS) quoted in older textbooks.** CVS carries a 1–2% chance of **confined placental mosaicism**, which amniocentesis does not.

**Increased NT ≥3.5 mm** with a normal karyotype/NIPT still warrants **detailed anatomy scan, fetal echocardiography and chromosomal microarray** — the association is with cardiac defects and genetic syndromes, not only aneuploidy. ⚑ **Chromosomal microarray has replaced karyotype as first-line** when a structural anomaly is present, because it detects copy-number variants that karyotype misses.

Rh-negative women undergoing CVS or amniocentesis require anti-D (dose by gestation as above).

## 3.5 Gestational diabetes screening ⚑

⚑ **The ADIPS 2025 consensus raised all three diagnostic thresholds.** Australian laboratories transitioned during 2025 (Victoria state-wide from 4 August 2025). Older notes quoting 5.1/10.0/8.5 are now out of date.

| 75 g OGTT at 24–28 weeks | **ADIPS 2025** | ADIPS 2014 (superseded) |
|---|---|---|
| Fasting | **≥5.3 mmol/L** | ≥5.1 |
| 1 hour | **≥10.6 mmol/L** | ≥10.0 |
| 2 hour | **≥9.0 mmol/L** | ≥8.5 |

One abnormal value is sufficient. Values above ~7.0 fasting / 11.1 at 2 h suggest **overt diabetes in pregnancy**, not GDM.

Early testing: risk factors (prior GDM, BMI ≥30, age ≥30, family history, PCOS, high-risk ethnicity, previous macrosomia) → **first-trimester HbA1c**; **prior GDM or HbA1c 6.0–6.4% → OGTT before 20 weeks, ideally 10–14 weeks**. Treatment targets: **fasting <5.3, 1 h post-prandial <7.8, or 2 h post-prandial <6.7 mmol/L**.

**Do not screen with urinalysis** — glycosuria is physiological (Section 1.7).

## 3.6 Group B streptococcus

**Australia has no national consensus.** RANZCOG accepts **either** universal culture-based screening **or** a risk-factor-based approach, and services follow local policy. ⚑ Contrast: **the US (CDC/ACOG) mandates universal screening; the UK (RCOG) and New Zealand use risk-based.** Saying "universal screening is standard" is only half-right in an Australian answer.

- **Swab at 35–37 weeks**: a single swab taken **low vaginal then anorectal** (introitus first, then anus), into standard transport medium.
- **Indications for intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis (IAP):** positive GBS swab; **GBS bacteriuria at any point in this pregnancy**; **previous infant with invasive GBS disease** (both of the latter override swab results); preterm labour <37 weeks; **ROM ≥18 hours**; intrapartum fever ≥38 °C.
- **Regimen:** **benzylpenicillin 3 g IV loading dose, then 1.8 g IV 4-hourly** until birth. Non-severe penicillin allergy: **cephazolin 2 g then 1 g 8-hourly**. Anaphylaxis: **clindamycin 900 mg 8-hourly** (or vancomycin if resistance unknown).
- **"Adequate" prophylaxis = commenced ≥4 hours before birth** — the figure that determines neonatal observation and workup.
- **Elective caesarean with intact membranes and not in labour does not require GBS IAP** (surgical prophylaxis is a separate indication).

## 3.7 Vaccination in pregnancy

| Vaccine | Timing | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Influenza (inactivated)** | **any trimester, every pregnancy** | NIP-funded; reduces stillbirth, preterm birth and severe maternal illness |
| **dTpa** | **20–32 weeks, every pregnancy** | ⚑ Australian window is **20–32 weeks** (UK: 16–32). Timing optimises transplacental IgG transfer to protect the infant until their 6-week dose |
| **RSV (RSVpreF, Abrysvo)** | ⚑ **28–36 weeks**, single dose **each pregnancy** | ⚑ **New — NIP-funded from 2025.** Registered from 24 weeks but **not routinely recommended before 28 weeks**. ~70% reduction in severe infant RSV disease. Alternative infant pathway: nirsevimab |
| **COVID-19 (mRNA)** | any trimester | |
| Others as indicated | any trimester | hepatitis A/B, pneumococcal, meningococcal, tetanus, rabies post-exposure — all inactivated, all safe |

**Contraindicated — live attenuated vaccines:** **MMR, varicella, zoster (live formulation), BCG, live oral typhoid, yellow fever** (relative — may be given if travel is unavoidable and risk is high).

- Advise avoiding pregnancy for **28 days** after MMR or varicella.
- **Inadvertent administration of a live vaccine in pregnancy is NOT an indication for termination** — actual fetal harm has not been demonstrated. This is a favourite exam point.
- **Non-immune to rubella or varicella → vaccinate postpartum before discharge.** Live vaccines are safe while breastfeeding.

## 3.8 Nutrition and supplementation

| Nutrient | Dose | Timing / notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Folic acid** | **400–500 µg/day** | from **≥1 month preconception to 12 weeks** |
| **High-dose folic acid** | **5 mg/day** | previous NTD-affected pregnancy, family history of NTD, **pre-existing diabetes**, **BMI >30**, antiepileptics (valproate, carbamazepine), other folate antagonists (methotrexate, trimethoprim, sulfasalazine), malabsorption/bariatric surgery |
| **Iodine** | **150 µg/day** | preconception, pregnancy and lactation. Australia has mandatory iodine fortification of bread, but supplementation is still recommended. Seek advice first in known thyroid disease |
| **Vitamin B12** | **2.6 µg/day** (2.8 lactation) | ⚑ **essential in vegans/strict vegetarians** |
| Vitamin D | 1000 IU if 25(OH)D 30–49 nmol/L; **2000 IU if <30 nmol/L** | risk-based testing; recheck at 28 weeks |
| Calcium | 1000 mg/day (1300 mg if ≤18 y) | supplement reduces pre-eclampsia risk where intake is low |
| Iron | **not routine** | treat if Hb below threshold or **ferritin <30 µg/L**; ~100 mg elemental daily. ⚑ **alternate-day dosing improves absorption** (hepcidin kinetics). IV iron generally avoided in the first trimester |

**Clue → answer:** an exclusively breastfed infant of a **vegan mother** with developmental regression, hypotonia and macrocytic anaemia = **maternal/infant B12 deficiency**. This is the classic reason B12 is asked about, and why B12 status must be checked and supplemented in vegan pregnancies rather than assumed adequate.

**Food and lifestyle:** avoid listeria risks (soft/unpasteurised cheese, pre-prepared salads, deli meats, pâté, cold smoked seafood); avoid high-mercury fish (**shark/flake, swordfish, broadbill, marlin — no more than one serve per fortnight**, FSANZ); **no safe level of alcohol (NHMRC — abstinence)**; caffeine **≤200 mg/day**. Recommended gestational weight gain (IOM ranges): underweight 12.5–18 kg; normal BMI 11.5–16 kg; overweight 7–11.5 kg; obese 5–9 kg.

## 3.9 Fetal growth surveillance

**Symphysis-fundal height** from **24 weeks** at every visit, measured in cm with the tape reversed to blind the measurer, and **plotted on a customised (e.g. GROW) chart** rather than judged against "cm ≈ weeks ± 2–3". Sensitivity for SGA is poor (~30% alone) — it is a trigger for imaging, not a test.

**Serial growth ultrasound** (typically 4-weekly from 26–28 weeks) is indicated for: previous SGA baby or stillbirth, chronic hypertension or pre-eclampsia, **pre-gestational diabetes**, smoking, maternal age >40, BMI extremes, **low PAPP-A (<0.4 MoM)**, abnormal uterine artery Dopplers, APH, multiple pregnancy, renal disease, SLE/APS. Also whenever SFH plots off-centile or palpation is unreliable (obesity, fibroids, polyhydramnios).

Definitions and surveillance:
- **SGA** = EFW or AC **<10th customised centile**. **FGR** = **<3rd centile**, or <10th centile **with abnormal Dopplers or poor interval growth**.
- ⚑ **Umbilical artery Doppler is the only surveillance tool proven to reduce perinatal mortality in high-risk pregnancies** — but it is **useless as a screening test in low-risk women.** Deterioration sequence: raised PI → **absent end-diastolic flow → reversed end-diastolic flow**.
- **MCA Doppler / cerebroplacental ratio** detects "brain sparing" and is more useful in **late-onset** FGR, where umbilical artery Dopplers often remain normal.
- **Ductus venosus a-wave loss or reversal** is a late sign of cardiac decompensation in **early-onset** FGR and, with the TRUFFLE approach, guides timing of delivery before 32 weeks.
- **Reduced fetal movements** → same-day assessment: CTG plus ultrasound for growth and liquor. ⚑ **Formal kick-counting charts are no longer recommended** — the AFFIRM trial showed no reduction in stillbirth and increased obstetric intervention. Advise awareness of *change*, not counting.
- **Routine third-trimester ultrasound in low-risk pregnancy is not recommended** by RANZCOG; some Australian units nevertheless offer a ~36-week scan for presentation and growth, and practice varies.

## Rapid-Recall Discriminators

| Clue / question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Australian routine antenatal anti-D regimen | **625 IU IM at 28 AND 34 weeks** (UK: 1500 IU once at 28, or 500 IU ×2) |
| Anti-D dose for a sensitising event at 9 weeks vs 20 weeks | **250 IU** vs **625 IU** |
| Anti-D already-formed anti-D on antibody screen | **Do not give anti-D** — refer MFM, titres + **MCA PSV >1.5 MoM = fetal anaemia** |
| RhD-negative woman, fetal RhD NIPT negative at 16 weeks | **No antenatal anti-D required** ⚑ (Lifeblood, MBS-funded, from 15 weeks) |
| Postnatal test required in every RhD-neg woman with RhD-pos baby | **Kleihauer/flow cytometry for FMH volume** |
| Window for NT measurement and valid CRL range | **11+0–13+6 weeks, CRL 45–84 mm** |
| "No-call" NIPT in a woman with BMI 42 — and why it matters | **Low fetal fraction**; also seen with **T13/T18/triploidy** — repeat or offer diagnostic testing, do not reassure |
| NIPT funding in Australia | ⚑ **Not MBS-funded** (~$400–500 out of pocket) |
| Procedure-related loss rate for amniocentesis | ⚑ **~0.1–0.2% above background**, not the 1% of older texts |
| First-line genetic test when a structural anomaly is seen | ⚑ **Chromosomal microarray**, not karyotype |
| Current Australian GDM thresholds (75 g OGTT) | ⚑ **ADIPS 2025: 5.3 / 10.6 / 9.0 mmol/L** (was 5.1 / 10.0 / 8.5) |
| GBS swab timing and site | **35–37 weeks, low vaginal + anorectal, one swab** |
| Is universal GBS screening standard in Australia? | ⚑ **No** — RANZCOG accepts universal **or** risk-based. US = universal, UK/NZ = risk-based |
| GBS IAP regimen and "adequate" duration | **Benzylpenicillin 3 g then 1.8 g q4h**; **≥4 h before birth** |
| GBS bacteriuria this pregnancy, negative 36-week swab | **Still give IAP** — bacteriuria and previous invasive GBS override the swab |
| dTpa timing in Australia | **20–32 weeks, every pregnancy** (UK: 16–32) |
| RSV vaccine in pregnancy | ⚑ **28–36 weeks, single dose each pregnancy, NIP-funded** |
| MMR given inadvertently at 7 weeks | **Not an indication for termination** — reassure, no demonstrated fetal harm |
| Rubella non-immune at booking | **Vaccinate postpartum before discharge**; safe with breastfeeding |
| Indications for 5 mg folate | Previous NTD, family history, **pre-existing diabetes**, **BMI >30**, antiepileptics/folate antagonists, malabsorption |
| Vegan mother, breastfed infant with regression + hypotonia | **B12 deficiency** — supplement 2.6 µg/day in pregnancy |
| Only fetal surveillance tool proven to reduce perinatal mortality | ⚑ **Umbilical artery Doppler in high-risk pregnancies** (not in low-risk screening) |
| Reduced fetal movements — recommend kick charts? | ⚑ **No** (AFFIRM). Assess same day with CTG + USS |
| Small fetus on a 34-week scan — re-date? | **Never re-date in the third trimester** — this is FGR until disproven |

### Sources
- National Blood Authority, *Guideline for the Prophylactic Use of Rh D Immunoglobulin in Pregnancy Care* (2024) and Guideline Summary; Queensland Clinical Guidelines, *Rh D Negative Women and Pregnancy* short guide.
- Australian Red Cross Lifeblood, *Fetal Blood Group Testing* (RhD NIPT, from 15 weeks, MBS bulk-billed).
- Australian Immunisation Handbook, *Vaccination for Women Planning Pregnancy, Pregnant or Breastfeeding*; Immunisation Coalition, *Vaccination During Pregnancy Guide* (dTpa 20–32 weeks; RSV 28–36 weeks).
- ADIPS 2025 consensus recommendations (*MJA* 2025;223:3) and Australian Prescriber / Safer Care Victoria implementation summaries.
- RANZCOG, *Prenatal Screening and Diagnostic Testing for Fetal Chromosomal and Genetic Conditions* (with HGSA); *Routine Antenatal Assessment in the Absence of Pregnancy Complications* (C-Obs 3b); *Vitamin and Mineral Supplementation in Pregnancy* (C-Obs 25); *Maternal Group B Streptococcus in Pregnancy: Screening and Management*.
- Safer Care Victoria, *Group B Streptococcus (GBS) — Screening and Management*; O&G Magazine, *Group B Streptococcus* (Australian vs international screening strategies).
- RACGP, *Guidelines for Preventive Activities in General Practice* (Red Book) — first antenatal visit.
- Australian Pregnancy Care Guidelines (Department of Health).


---

# O&G Notes — Section 4: Medical Disorders of Pregnancy
*Australian-anchored: SOMANZ 2023 (hypertension), SOMANZ 2023 (ICP), SOMANZ 2020 (NVP), ADIPS 2025 (GDM), Queensland Clinical Guidelines (HDP, VTE), RANZCOG C-Obs 46, Therapeutic Guidelines, Australian Prescriber. Scope = written finals: recognition, thresholds, drug-of-choice, timing-of-birth decisions. UK/NICE divergences are flagged where they are exam-relevant.*

---

## 4.1 Hypertensive disorders — the classification comes first

Every hypertensive vignette is answered by placing the woman into one of four boxes. Hypertension in pregnancy = **sBP ≥140 and/or dBP ≥90** on two occasions (or once if severe), Korotkoff V for diastolic.

| Box | Defining feature | Discriminator |
|---|---|---|
| Chronic (pre-existing) HTN | HTN **before 20/40** or pre-dating pregnancy | Present at booking; doesn't resolve by 3/12 postpartum |
| Gestational HTN | New HTN **after 20/40**, **no** end-organ features | Diagnosis of exclusion; **~1 in 4 progress to pre-eclampsia**, so it is never "reassure and discharge" |
| Pre-eclampsia (PE) | New HTN after 20/40 **plus ≥1 new maternal organ or uteroplacental feature** | **Proteinuria is not required** ⚑ — a normotensive-protein-free woman with new thrombocytopenia + RUQ pain + FGR is still PE |
| Superimposed PE | Chronic HTN **plus** new organ dysfunction / step-up in BP / new proteinuria | Hardest to call — look for a *new* feature, not just worse numbers |

**White-coat HTN** (raised in clinic, normal on ABPM/HBPM) still carries elevated PE risk — monitor, don't dismiss.

**Organ features that make it pre-eclampsia** (SOMANZ 2023 / QCG):
- **Renal** — protein:creatinine ratio **≥30 mg/mmol**, creatinine **≥90 µmol/L**, oliguria
- **Haematological** — thrombocytopenia **<150 ×10⁹/L** (SOMANZ 2023 / QCG diagnostic threshold — note this is *not* the <100 used to define **severe** disease below), haemolysis, DIC
- **Hepatic** — transaminases **>40 IU/L** (or >2× ULN), severe persistent RUQ/epigastric pain
- **Neurological** — new severe headache, visual scotomata/photopsia, hyperreflexia with **sustained clonus**, altered GCS, stroke, eclampsia
- **Pulmonary** — pulmonary oedema
- **Uteroplacental** — FGR, abnormal umbilical artery Dopplers, abruption, stillbirth

**Severe hypertension = ≥160 systolic and/or ≥110 diastolic.** This is a *number*, not a syndrome — a woman can have severe HTN without severe PE and vice versa. Treat the number urgently regardless.

**The short version**
- Proteinuria is optional; end-organ involvement is not.
- Gestational HTN is a holding diagnosis — 25% become PE.
- ≥160/110 is severe hypertension and needs treatment within minutes, not hours.

---

## 4.2 Prevention and prediction

- **Aspirin** — the single highest-yield intervention. **Start before 16/40**, take at **night**, continue to ~36 weeks. SOMANZ 2023 says **150 mg daily**; QCG allows **100–150 mg**. ⚑ The old "75–100 mg" advice was superseded by 150 mg on ASPRE data — quote 150 mg for a single number. Indicated for previous PE, chronic HTN, pre-gestational diabetes, CKD, SLE/APS, or **≥2 moderate risks** (nulliparity, age ≥40, BMI ≥30, family history, multiple pregnancy). Aspirin started at 24 weeks is of little value.
- **Calcium** 1.2–2.5 g/day if dietary intake **<1 g/day**.
- **sFlt-1/PlGF ratio** — a **rule-out** test: ratio **≤38** makes PE within the next week very unlikely (high NPV). ⚑ Not universally available in Australia; never use it to *diagnose* PE.

---

## 4.3 Pre-eclampsia with severe features, eclampsia and HELLP

**Severe PE** = PE plus any of: sBP ≥160/dBP ≥110 despite treatment, platelets <100, transaminases >2× ULN with RUQ pain, creatinine rising, pulmonary oedema, persistent neurological signs, eclampsia. Severe PE <34 weeks is the classic "stabilise, steroids, magnesium, transfer to tertiary, then deliver" stem.

**Eclampsia** — tonic-clonic seizure in a woman with PE. Clues that a seizure is eclamptic rather than epileptic: gestation >20/40 or **within 48 h postpartum**, preceding headache/visual change/RUQ pain, hyperreflexia and clonus, raised BP (though **up to ~15% are normotensive at the time of fit** — do not exclude eclampsia on a normal BP). Management sequence:
1. ABC, left lateral, oxygen, protect airway — **do not** give diazepam first-line.
2. **MgSO₄** 4 g IV over 20 min, then 1 g/h.
3. Recurrent seizure → **further 2 g IV over 5 min** (QCG allows repeat after 2 min).
4. Control BP to <160/110.
5. Deliver **once the mother is stable** — the fetus is not the emergency; maternal stabilisation is. Delivery is the definitive treatment.

**HELLP** — Haemolysis, Elevated Liver enzymes, Low Platelets. It is a severe variant of PE, and **12–18% are normotensive**, which is what makes it a trap.
- Haemolysis: ↑LDH, ↓haptoglobin, **schistocytes/fragments on film**, ↑unconjugated bilirubin
- ↑AST/ALT (>2× ULN)
- Platelets **<100 ×10⁹/L**
- Presentation: RUQ/epigastric pain, nausea/vomiting, malaise — frequently misfiled as gastritis or biliary colic. Complication to know: **subcapsular hepatic haematoma → rupture** (sudden shoulder-tip pain, shock).
- Management = MgSO₄ + BP control + **delivery**. Expectant management is dangerous (QCG cites ~6.3% maternal mortality). **Corticosteroids do not improve maternal outcomes in HELLP** ⚑ — give betamethasone only for fetal lung maturation, not to "treat the platelets".

**Confusable trio at term with RUQ pain + deranged LFTs**

| | Discriminator |
|---|---|
| **HELLP** | Platelets ↓↓, haemolysis, hypertension usually present, glucose normal |
| **Acute fatty liver of pregnancy** | **Hypoglycaemia**, ↑ammonia, coagulopathy out of proportion, ↑urate, often *nauseated + polydipsic*; Swansea criteria |
| **Obstetric cholestasis** | **Itch without rash** dominates, bile acids ↑↑, BP and platelets normal |

**The short version**
- Normotensive HELLP and normotensive eclampsia both exist — pattern beats blood pressure.
- Magnesium prevents/treats seizures; it is not an antihypertensive.
- Delivery cures PE. Everything else buys time.

---

## 4.4 Drugs: antihypertensives and magnesium

**Acute severe hypertension (≥160/110) — treat within 15–30 min.** Target sBP 130–150, dBP 80–90; avoid precipitous drops (uteroplacental hypoperfusion).

| Agent | Dose | Onset | Note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Nifedipine IR** oral | 10–20 mg, repeat at 45 min, max 80 mg/day | 30–45 min | Practical first choice on a ward; **do not** give sublingual |
| **Labetalol** IV | 20 mg, then 40–80 mg q10 min, max 300 mg/24 h | ~5 min | Avoid in asthma, decompensated heart failure |
| **Hydralazine** IV | 5–10 mg over 3–10 min, repeat q20 min, max 30 mg | ~20 min | Preload-dependent — **co-load with fluid**; causes reflex tachycardia and headache (mimics PE symptoms) |

**Ongoing oral therapy** — start when BP persistently ≥140/90; SOMANZ 2023 target **≤135/85** ⚑ (tighter than the older "treat only if ≥160/100" teaching, following CHIPS/CHAP).

| Agent | Typical dosing | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Labetalol** | 100 mg BD → 200–400 mg BD–QID (max 2.4 g/day) | Usual first line; asthma is the limiter; watch neonatal hypoglycaemia/bradycardia |
| **Methyldopa** | 125–250 mg BD → max 2 g/day | Longest safety record; sedation, depression — **avoid postpartum** (depression risk); slow onset |
| **Nifedipine SR** | 20–30 mg daily → max 120 mg/day | Headache, flushing, ankle oedema |
| **Hydralazine oral** | 25 mg BD → max 200 mg/day | Add-on |

**Contraindicated / avoid**: **ACE inhibitors and ARBs** (fetal renal dysgenesis, oligohydramnios, skull hypoplasia — safe postpartum and in breastfeeding, e.g. enalapril/captopril), **atenolol** (FGR with prolonged use), routine **diuretics** (plasma volume already contracted), **direct renin inhibitors**.

**Magnesium sulfate**
- **Indications**: eclampsia (treatment), severe PE (seizure prophylaxis), and **fetal neuroprotection when birth is imminent <30 weeks** (Australian practice; some centres extend to <32 weeks — the ANZ guidance is <30). ⚑ Neuroprotective use is a separate indication with the same drug.
- **Regimen**: 4 g IV over 20 min → **1 g/h for 24 h** (24 h from the last seizure or from birth, whichever is later). Renal impairment → maintenance 0.5 g/h with levels.
- **Monitoring**: hourly respiratory rate, deep tendon reflexes, urine output; continuous BP/pulse initially. Therapeutic range ~1.7–3.5 mmol/L.
- **Toxicity sequence** (useful MCQ ladder): loss of patellar reflexes → respiratory depression → cardiac arrest. **Antidote: calcium gluconate 1 g IV.** First sign to be lost is the deep tendon reflex — this is why reflexes are checked hourly.
- Magnesium **lowers BP only modestly** — never rely on it for BP control.

**Postpartum**: BP typically **peaks day 3–6**, so discharge with a BP plan. NSAIDs are usually avoided in PE with renal impairment or persistent hypertension. Women with PE carry roughly a **2–4× lifetime cardiovascular risk** — arrange 6–12 week review, and lifelong CV risk-factor management (SOMANZ 2023 places new emphasis on this).

---

## 4.5 Timing of birth in hypertensive disease

| Scenario | Australian approach |
|---|---|
| Gestational HTN, well controlled | Birth from ~**37–38 weeks** |
| Pre-eclampsia **≥37+0** | **Deliver** (SOMANZ 2023: initiate birth) |
| Pre-eclampsia 34+0–36+6 | Individualise; expectant with close surveillance is reasonable if stable |
| Pre-eclampsia **<34 weeks**, stable | Expectant in a tertiary unit + **betamethasone**; prolongs gestation, improves neonatal outcome |
| **Severe PE / eclampsia / HELLP / AFLP** at any gestation | Deliver — stabilise first, but **do not delay** |
| Non-reassuring fetal status, abruption, uncontrollable BP, pulmonary oedema, deteriorating renal or hepatic function | Deliver |

Mode of birth is obstetric, not dictated by PE per se — vaginal birth is appropriate if the cervix is favourable and the fetus is well.

---

## 4.6 Gestational diabetes — ADIPS 2025 ⚑⚑ (highest-yield change in this section)

**Screening (ADIPS 2025)**
- **First antenatal visit**: HbA1c if risk factors (previous GDM, obesity, first-degree family history, age ≥30, PCOS, hypothyroidism, adverse obstetric history, high-risk ethnicity, corticosteroid/antipsychotic use).
- **Early 75 g OGTT before 20 weeks (ideally 10–14 weeks)** if: previous GDM, **or** early HbA1c **6.0–6.4% (42–47 mmol/mol)**. ⚑ Early OGTT is now *targeted*, no longer offered broadly.
- **Universal 75 g OGTT at 24–28 weeks** for everyone not already diagnosed.

**Diagnostic thresholds — 75 g OGTT, any ONE value diagnoses GDM**

| | **ADIPS 2025 (current)** ⚑ | ADIPS 2014 (superseded) |
|---|---|---|
| Fasting | **≥5.3 mmol/L** (5.3–6.9) | ≥5.1 mmol/L (5.1–6.9) |
| 1 hour | **≥10.6 mmol/L** | ≥10.0 mmol/L |
| 2 hour | **≥9.0 mmol/L** (9.0–11.0) | ≥8.5 mmol/L (8.5–11.0) |

*Every threshold went **up**. Mnemonic for the new set: **5.3 / 10.6 / 9.0** — note 10.6 is exactly double 5.3.* The rationale was overdiagnosis under IADPSG-derived criteria, informed by **TOBOGM** (early treatment benefits the higher-glycaemia band) and **GEMS** (higher thresholds did not worsen outcomes overall). Older textbooks and many hospital handbooks still print 5.1/10.0/8.5 — if an exam question is dated, read the stem for which criteria it is using.

**Overt diabetes in pregnancy (overt DIP)** — not GDM, manage as pre-existing diabetes: **FPG ≥7.0**, or **2 h ≥11.1**, or **HbA1c ≥6.5% (≥48 mmol/mol)**.
**Early GDM** = GDM criteria met **before 20 weeks**.

**Management ladder**
1. **Lifestyle first** — dietitian-led medical nutrition therapy (carbohydrate quantity/quality/distribution), physical activity, gestational weight-gain targets, plus **self-monitored BGL** 4×/day (fasting + post-prandial). Roughly 70–80% are managed on lifestyle alone.
2. **Pharmacotherapy if targets are not met** (typically after 1–2 weeks of lifestyle). **In Australia, insulin is first-line pharmacotherapy** — usually basal (isophane/detemir) for fasting hyperglycaemia, rapid-acting prandial for post-prandial peaks, or both.
3. **Metformin** — crosses the placenta; ADIPS does not endorse it for routine first-line use, reserving it for individualised situations (e.g. severe insulin refusal/inability, marked insulin resistance) usually **with** insulin. ⚑ **This is where Australia diverges from the UK: NICE recommends metformin first-line, adding insulin if targets are unmet.** If an exam stem is British in flavour, metformin is the answer; in an Australian paper, insulin is.
4. **Glibenclamide** is essentially obsolete in Australian practice.

**Glycaemic targets** — sources genuinely disagree; know the tighter set and be aware of the looser:
- Traditional ADIPS/Australian hospital targets: fasting **≤5.0**, 1 h post-prandial **≤7.4**, 2 h **≤6.7 mmol/L**.
- *Australian Prescriber* (2025) quotes fasting **<5.3**, 1 h **<7.8**, 2 h **<6.7 mmol/L**.
The 2 h figure (6.7) is consistent across both; the fasting and 1 h figures differ. Local protocol wins in practice.

**Consequences**: macrosomia with **asymmetric growth (abdominal circumference)**, polyhydramnios, shoulder dystocia and brachial plexus injury, pre-eclampsia, preterm birth, stillbirth, caesarean; neonatal **hypoglycaemia**, hyperbilirubinaemia, polycythaemia, respiratory distress. Serial growth scans from ~28–30 weeks if insulin-requiring or poorly controlled.

**Timing of birth**: diet-controlled and well grown → expectant to **40–41 weeks**; insulin-requiring or complicated → **38–39 weeks**. Intrapartum, keep BGL ~4–7 mmol/L; insulin requirements collapse the moment the placenta delivers, so **stop GDM insulin at birth**.

**Postpartum**: **75 g OGTT at 6–12 weeks** using non-pregnant criteria, then HbA1c/FPG/OGTT **every 1–3 years lifelong** — 50–60% develop type 2 diabetes within a decade and CVD risk roughly doubles.

**The short version**
- 2025 numbers: **5.3 / 10.6 / 9.0** — all raised from 5.1/10.0/8.5. ⚑
- HbA1c ≥6.5% or fasting ≥7.0 or 2 h ≥11.1 = overt diabetes, not GDM.
- Australia = insulin first; NICE = metformin first.

---

## 4.7 VTE in pregnancy

Pregnancy is prothrombotic throughout and **maximally in the first 6 weeks postpartum**. VTE remains a leading direct cause of maternal death in Australia.

**Recognition**
- **DVT**: ~85% are **left-sided** and **iliofemoral** (compression of the left iliac vein by the right iliac artery and gravid uterus) — whole-leg swelling, back/buttock pain. Left leg swelling in pregnancy is DVT until excluded.
- **PE**: dyspnoea, pleuritic pain, tachycardia, hypoxia, collapse. **D-dimer is not useful** — physiologically raised in pregnancy; a negative result does not exclude PE and a positive result adds nothing. Go straight to imaging.
- Imaging: **compression duplex ultrasound** first for suspected DVT (and if positive with suspected PE, treat — no chest imaging needed). For suspected PE with normal leg dopplers: CXR first, then **V/Q (or perfusion-only) scan vs CTPA**. V/Q gives lower maternal breast dose; CTPA gives lower fetal dose and is better if the CXR is abnormal. Both fetal doses are far below teratogenic thresholds — **do not withhold imaging**.

**Treatment**: **LMWH (enoxaparin)** — does not cross the placenta, no routine monitoring. Antenatal therapeutic dose **1 mg/kg SC BD**; postnatal **1.5 mg/kg SC daily**. Continue for the remainder of the pregnancy **and at least 6 weeks postpartum**, minimum 3 months total. **Warfarin is teratogenic** (chondrodysplasia punctata, nasal hypoplasia at 6–12 weeks; fetal haemorrhage later) — stop before 6 weeks' gestation; it *is* safe in breastfeeding. **DOACs: avoid in pregnancy and breastfeeding.**

**Prophylaxis** (QCG risk scoring): antenatal LMWH if score ≥4, from the first trimester if previous VTE or high-risk thrombophilia; postnatal LMWH **7 days** if score ≥3, extended to **6 weeks** for previous unprovoked VTE, high-risk thrombophilia or recurrent VTE. Standard prophylactic enoxaparin is **40 mg daily** at booking weight 50–90 kg, scaled up by weight band.

**Neuraxial timing** (frequently examined): prophylactic LMWH — **12 h** before block; therapeutic LMWH — **24 h** before block.

---

## 4.8 Anaemia and iron

Physiological haemodilution drops Hb; the thresholds are trimester-specific.

| | Anaemia if Hb below |
|---|---|
| 1st trimester | **110 g/L** |
| 2nd trimester | **105 g/L** |
| 3rd trimester | 110 g/L (many Australian units use 105 g/L through the 2nd and 3rd) |
| Postpartum | **100 g/L** |

**Ferritin <30 µg/L = iron deficiency** (treat). Ferritin is an acute-phase reactant — a normal ferritin with raised CRP does not exclude deficiency; use transferrin saturation. Screen with FBC + ferritin at **booking, 24–28 weeks and ~36 weeks**.

**Treatment**: oral elemental iron 100 mg — **alternate-day dosing** is preferred where tolerated ⚑ (a dose triggers a hepcidin surge that blocks absorption for ~24 h, so alternate-day gives equal or better uptake with fewer GI effects). Take with vitamin C, away from tea/coffee/calcium/PPI; recheck at 2–4 weeks. **IV iron** (ferric carboxymaltose/derisomaltose) for oral intolerance or failure, or when time is short — restricted to the **2nd and 3rd trimesters**; causes **transient hypophosphataemia** and skin staining on extravasation.

Consider **thalassaemia trait** if MCV is low with normal/raised ferritin and a normal or high red cell count.

---

## 4.9 Thyroid disease in pregnancy

**Physiology**: hCG cross-stimulates the TSH receptor → **TSH falls in T1** while free T4 transiently rises; oestrogen raises thyroxine-binding globulin so total T4/T3 rise (use **free** T4). Iodine requirement rises — **all pregnant and breastfeeding women in Australia should take iodine 150 µg/day**.

**Reference ranges** (RANZCOG C-Obs 46): T1 upper limit is ~0.5 mU/L below the non-pregnant range; T2/T3 as non-pregnant. A pragmatic single figure is an upper limit of **4 mIU/L** throughout pregnancy.

**Hypothyroidism**
- **Overt** = ↑TSH with ↓free T4, **or TSH >10** with normal free T4 → treat.
- **Pre-existing on thyroxine**: increase the dose **as soon as pregnancy is confirmed** — the practical instruction is **two extra doses per week** (~a 25–30% increase; RANZCOG quotes 30–50%). Check TSH at least once per trimester; aim for the lower half of the trimester-specific range. Return to pre-pregnancy dose immediately postpartum.
- **Subclinical hypothyroidism (SCH)** — **genuine guideline disagreement**: **RANZCOG C-Obs 46 does not recommend treating SCH** (large RCTs showed no maternal or neonatal benefit). The ATA and *Australian Prescriber* support levothyroxine when TSH is 2.5–4 mIU/L **with positive TPO antibodies**, or TSH ≥4 regardless of antibodies. In an Australian written paper, follow RANZCOG; be able to state the alternative.
- **Screening**: **targeted, not universal** (symptoms, T1DM, personal/family thyroid history, previous neck irradiation, recurrent miscarriage). Routine TPO antibody screening is not recommended.

**Hyperthyroidism**
- Distinguish **gestational transient thyrotoxicosis** (hCG-driven, peaks 8–14 weeks, associated with hyperemesis, **no goitre, no eye signs, TRAb negative**, resolves by ~20 weeks — supportive care only) from **Graves' disease** (goitre, orbitopathy, **TRAb positive**, pre-dates pregnancy).
- **Drug choice by trimester**: **propylthiouracil in the first trimester** (carbimazole/methimazole → aplasia cutis, choanal/oesophageal atresia — "carbimazole embryopathy"); switch to **carbimazole from the second trimester** to avoid PTU hepatotoxicity. Use the lowest dose keeping free T4 at the upper limit of normal. **Do not** use block-and-replace in pregnancy. Radioiodine is absolutely contraindicated.
- **TRAb crosses the placenta** — measure in women with current or past Graves' (including post-thyroidectomy/radioiodine) at ~18–22 and 30–34 weeks; high titres → risk of **fetal/neonatal thyrotoxicosis** (fetal tachycardia >160, goitre, FGR) → fetal surveillance and neonatal thyroid checks.
- **Thyroid storm** is precipitated by labour, infection, caesarean — a life-threatening pregnancy emergency.

**Postpartum thyroiditis**: 5–10% of women, higher with TPO antibodies or T1DM. **Painless**, classically thyrotoxic phase at 1–4 months → hypothyroid at 4–8 months → usually euthyroid by 12 months. **Distinguish from Graves'**: postpartum thyroiditis has **low radioiodine uptake**, negative TRAb, no orbitopathy. Beta-blocker for the thyrotoxic phase (never antithyroid drugs — it is destructive, not overproductive); thyroxine if symptomatic hypothyroidism. Up to 20–30% become permanently hypothyroid — check TSH annually.

---

## 4.10 Epilepsy and teratogenic antiseizure medications

**Principles**: most women with epilepsy have uncomplicated pregnancies. **Seizures — especially generalised tonic-clonic — are more dangerous than most modern ASMs**, and SUDEP risk rises with non-adherence. Never stop an ASM abruptly because a woman is pregnant. Optimise **pre-conceptually**: monotherapy, lowest effective dose, **folic acid 5 mg daily** (high dose for all women on ASMs) from at least 1 month pre-conception through T1.

| Drug | Pregnancy risk | Verdict |
|---|---|---|
| **Sodium valproate** | Major congenital malformation **~3.3% at <1100 mg/day rising to ~30% at ≥1100 mg/day**; neural tube defects; **fetal valproate syndrome**; ↓IQ ~8–10 points; autism (OR 1.7–4.4), ADHD (OR 1.4–1.8), intellectual disability (OR 2.4–4.5) | **Avoid in anyone of childbearing potential** unless no alternative; TGA requires effective contraception be co-prescribed and risks documented. Dose-dependent, no safe dose ⚑ |
| **Topiramate** | **Oral clefts**; FGR/small for gestational age; emerging neurodevelopmental signal | Avoid — including when used for migraine or weight management ⚑ (a recently tightened warning that predates many textbooks) |
| **Carbamazepine** | Modest ↑ malformation (NTD, cardiac) | Second-tier; acceptable if effective and alternatives failed |
| **Phenytoin, phenobarbitone** | Fetal hydantoin syndrome; cognitive effects | Avoid where possible |
| **Lamotrigine** | Lowest malformation rates | **Preferred**. Levels fall substantially in pregnancy (oestrogen-induced glucuronidation) → **monitor levels and increase dose**, then reduce promptly postpartum to avoid toxicity |
| **Levetiracetam** | Lowest malformation rates | **Preferred**. Clearance also rises — monitor |

Also: detailed anomaly scan ± fetal echo; **all ASMs are compatible with breastfeeding**; postnatal safe-care advice (shower rather than bathe, change nappies on the floor, avoid co-sleeping and sleep deprivation). A seizure in the second half of pregnancy in a woman with no epilepsy history is **eclampsia until proven otherwise**.

---

## 4.11 Obstetric cholestasis (intrahepatic cholestasis of pregnancy, ICP)

**Clue → diagnosis**: **third-trimester itch, worst on palms and soles, worse at night, no primary rash** (excoriations only) + raised bile acids. Jaundice is uncommon (<10%). Risk factors: previous ICP (recurrence up to 90%), family history, multiple pregnancy, hepatitis C, IVF conception.

**Diagnosis (SOMANZ 2023)** — pruritus + elevated **total serum bile acids (TSBA)**, with other causes excluded.
- **Non-fasting TSBA ≥19 µmol/L** is the diagnostic threshold. ⚑ (Fasting ≥10 µmol/L is an alternative but is insensitive; the old textbook "any elevation above the lab range" is outdated.)
- Transaminases are often raised; **check for other causes** — viral hepatitis serology, EBV/CMV, liver autoantibodies, liver ultrasound, pre-eclampsia bloods.

**Severity and risk — the number that matters is 100** ⚑

| Band | Peak TSBA | Stillbirth risk | Planned birth |
|---|---|---|---|
| Mild | 19–39 µmol/L | Background | Not indicated for ICP alone; usually term |
| Severe | 40–99 µmol/L | ~0.28% (≈ background) | Consider **from 38+0** |
| Very severe | **≥100 µmol/L** | **~3.4%** (HR ~30), concentrated **35–36 weeks** | Consider **from 36+0** |

**Management**
- Repeat TSBA at least weekly in severe disease — decisions follow the **peak** value.
- **Ursodeoxycholic acid** — modest pruritus benefit and biochemical improvement, but **PITCHES showed no reduction in the composite perinatal outcome** ⚑: offer it for symptoms, do not promise it prevents stillbirth. Dose 500 mg/day (mild) up to 1500–2000 mg/day (severe, ~20 mg/kg). Second-line for refractory itch: rifampicin (under evaluation in TURRIFIC), plus emollients/menthol and a sedating antihistamine at night.
- **Vitamin K** 10 mg if PT/INR ≥1.4 or APTT ≥40 s (fat-soluble vitamin malabsorption → PPH and neonatal haemorrhage).
- CTG and ultrasound do **not** predict ICP stillbirth — reassuring surveillance must not delay planned birth in very severe disease.
- **Postnatal**: itch resolves within days; recheck **TSBA and ALT at 6 weeks** and investigate if still abnormal. Avoid the COCP until LFTs normalise. Counsel on high recurrence and long-term hepatobiliary risk.

**The short version**
- Itch without rash + bile acids **≥19** = ICP.
- **≥100 µmol/L** is the stillbirth-risk threshold → birth from 36 weeks.
- UDCA treats the mother's symptoms, not the fetal risk.

---

## Rapid-Recall Discriminators — Section 4

| Clue / question | Answer |
|---|---|
| New HTN at 32/40, no proteinuria, platelets 78, ALT 90 | Pre-eclampsia — **proteinuria is not required** |
| Gestational HTN — chance of progressing to PE | ~25% |
| Severe hypertension threshold in pregnancy | **≥160 systolic and/or ≥110 diastolic** |
| Target BP once treating hypertension in pregnancy (SOMANZ 2023) | **≤135/85** |
| Aspirin for PE prophylaxis — dose and timing | **150 mg nocte (100–150 mg), started before 16 weeks**, continue to ~36 weeks |
| PCR threshold for significant proteinuria | **≥30 mg/mmol** |
| MgSO₄ regimen | **4 g IV over 20 min, then 1 g/h for 24 h**; recurrent fit → 2 g over 5 min |
| First clinical sign of magnesium toxicity | **Loss of deep tendon reflexes** → antidote **calcium gluconate 1 g IV** |
| Seizure at 34/40, BP 118/74, hyperreflexic, preceding headache | Still **eclampsia** — ~15% are normotensive |
| Term woman, RUQ pain, ALT 300, platelets 60, **glucose 2.1** | **Acute fatty liver of pregnancy**, not HELLP (hypoglycaemia is the discriminator) |
| Steroids to treat the thrombocytopenia of HELLP | **No** — no maternal benefit; steroids are for fetal lungs only |
| GDM diagnostic thresholds, ADIPS 2025 | **Fasting ≥5.3, 1 h ≥10.6, 2 h ≥9.0 mmol/L** (any one) ⚑ |
| Same three under ADIPS 2014 | 5.1 / 10.0 / 8.5 — **all raised in 2025** |
| Values meaning overt diabetes in pregnancy, not GDM | FPG ≥7.0, 2 h ≥11.1, or **HbA1c ≥6.5%** |
| Who gets an early OGTT under ADIPS 2025 | Previous GDM, **or** booking **HbA1c 6.0–6.4%** — at 10–14 weeks |
| First-line drug for GDM in Australia vs UK | **Insulin** (Australia/ADIPS) vs **metformin** (NICE) ⚑ |
| Postpartum test after GDM | **75 g OGTT at 6–12 weeks**, then 1–3 yearly for life |
| D-dimer in suspected PE in pregnancy | **Not useful** — image instead |
| Side and level of the typical pregnancy DVT | **Left**, iliofemoral (~85%) |
| Therapeutic enoxaparin dosing in pregnancy | **1 mg/kg BD** antenatally; 1.5 mg/kg daily postnatally; continue ≥6 weeks postpartum |
| Stop prophylactic vs therapeutic LMWH before neuraxial block | **12 h** vs **24 h** |
| Anticoagulant that is teratogenic but breastfeeding-safe | **Warfarin** |
| Hb defining anaemia in the 2nd trimester | **<105 g/L** (110 in T1/T3) |
| Ferritin cut-off for iron deficiency in pregnancy | **<30 µg/L** |
| Levothyroxine adjustment on confirming pregnancy | Increase ~25–30% — **two extra doses per week**, immediately |
| Treat subclinical hypothyroidism in pregnancy? | **RANZCOG: no**; ATA/*Aust Prescriber*: yes if TPO+ or TSH ≥4 — sources disagree |
| Antithyroid drug by trimester | **PTU in T1** (carbimazole embryopathy), **carbimazole from T2** (PTU hepatotoxicity) |
| Hyperemesis + thyrotoxic biochemistry at 10/40, no goitre, TRAb negative | **Gestational transient thyrotoxicosis** — supportive only |
| Painless thyrotoxicosis 3 months postpartum, low uptake | **Postpartum thyroiditis** — beta-blocker, not carbimazole |
| Safest ASMs in pregnancy | **Lamotrigine and levetiracetam** — and both need dose *increases* as pregnancy progresses |
| ASM with dose-dependent malformation risk reaching ~30% | **Sodium valproate** (≥1100 mg/day) |
| ASM associated with oral clefts and FGR | **Topiramate** ⚑ |
| Folic acid dose on any ASM | **5 mg daily** |
| Third-trimester itch of palms and soles, no rash | **ICP** — TSBA **≥19 µmol/L** (non-fasting) |
| Bile acid level that changes delivery timing | **≥100 µmol/L** → plan birth from **36+0**; 40–99 → from 38+0 |
| Does UDCA prevent stillbirth in ICP | **No** (PITCHES) — symptom/biochemistry benefit only ⚑ |

### Sources
- SOMANZ. *Hypertension in Pregnancy Guideline 2023*; Shanmugalingam R et al., summary, **Med J Aust** 2024;220(11).
- Queensland Clinical Guidelines. *Hypertensive disorders of pregnancy* (MN.021) and *Venous thromboembolism prophylaxis in pregnancy and the puerperium* (MN.020); Queensland Health.
- Sweeting A et al. *Australasian Diabetes in Pregnancy Society (ADIPS) 2025 consensus recommendations for the screening, diagnosis and classification of gestational diabetes.* **Med J Aust** 2025;223(3). Plus ADIPS *FAQ for health professionals*, 23 June 2025, and the AACB/RCPA laboratory implementation guide, July 2025.
- ADIPS. *Consensus Guidelines for the Testing and Diagnosis of Hyperglycaemia in Pregnancy*, 2014 (superseded — retained here for the ⚑ comparison).
- *Gestational diabetes: update on screening, diagnosis and maternal management.* **Australian Prescriber** 2025.
- SOMANZ. *Intrahepatic Cholestasis of Pregnancy — Diagnosis and Management: Consensus Statement*, 2023 (Hague WM et al., **ANZJOG** 2023). Cross-checked against RCOG Green-top 43 (2022).
- RANZCOG. *Subclinical hypothyroidism and hypothyroidism in pregnancy* (C-Obs 46); *Thyroid disorders in pregnancy and postpartum*, **Australian Prescriber**.
- *Sodium valproate: balancing benefits and risks especially in people of childbearing potential.* **Australian Prescriber**; TGA safety advisories on valproate and topiramate.
- King Edward Memorial Hospital (WA) Clinical Guidelines: *Anaemia and iron deficiency in pregnancy and postpartum*; *Venous thromboembolism*.
- Therapeutic Guidelines (eTG) — obstetric medicine topics.

# O&G Notes — Section 5: Early Pregnancy Problems
*Australian-anchored: RANZCOG C-Gyn 38 (miscarriage, recurrent miscarriage, ectopic), Queensland Clinical Guidelines (Early pregnancy loss), RANZCOG GTD statement, SOMANZ NVP/HG guideline, state termination-of-pregnancy legislation. Scope: bleeding and pain <20 weeks, plus HG and TOP.*

---

## 5.1 The first move in any early-pregnancy bleed

Three questions, in order: **is she shocked**, **where is the pregnancy**, **is it viable**. Everything else follows.
- Resuscitate first. **Cervical shock** — bradycardia and hypotension from products in the cervical os — is relieved by removing the tissue with sponge forceps; it mimics haemorrhagic shock but the pulse is *slow*.
- **Rh D negative** → anti-D. **250 IU up to 12 weeks; 625 IU beyond 12 weeks**, within 72 h. ⚑ RANZCOG C-Gyn 38 states anti-D is **no longer routinely recommended for threatened miscarriage or miscarriage before 10 weeks** on evidence grounds — many Australian units still give it, so know both. Anti-D **should** be offered for surgically managed ectopic pregnancy.

**Ultrasound definitions of failed pregnancy** (transvaginal; conservative thresholds — when in doubt, rescan in 7–10 days):
- **MSD ≥25 mm with no embryo** = failed pregnancy
- **CRL ≥7 mm with no fetal heart** (observed ≥30 s) = failed pregnancy
- No embryo ≥2 weeks after a scan showing a sac without yolk sac, or ≥11 days after a sac with yolk sac
Never diagnose miscarriage on a single borderline scan, and never on hCG alone.

---

## 5.2 Miscarriage

Pregnancy loss before **20 weeks** (Australian definition; the UK uses 24 weeks ⚑). Occurs in ~15–20% of clinically recognised pregnancies; **50–60% are chromosomal**, mostly autosomal trisomy.

| Type | Bleeding | Pain | Os | Ultrasound | Discriminator |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Threatened** | Light | Mild/none | **Closed** | Viable IUP, FH present | Pregnancy still alive; ~50% continue |
| **Inevitable** | Heavy | Cramping | **Open** | Sac may still be in utero | Open os = inevitable, whatever the scan shows |
| **Incomplete** | Ongoing | Cramping | Open | **Retained products** | Bleeding persists because tissue remains |
| **Complete** | Settling | Settling | Closed | **Empty uterus, thin endometrium**, with prior confirmed IUP | Beware — an "empty uterus" without prior confirmed IUP may be an **ectopic** |
| **Missed (early fetal demise)** | None/spotting | None | Closed | CRL ≥7 mm, no FH, or MSD ≥25 mm empty | Often an incidental scan finding; symptoms absent |
| **Septic** | ± offensive discharge | Tender uterus | Open | RPOC | Fever, tachycardia, peritonism — resuscitate, IV antibiotics, **urgent evacuation** |

**Management — all three options are acceptable for a stable woman with incomplete or missed miscarriage; the choice is hers.**

| | Regimen | Best for | Caveats |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Expectant** | Wait 7–14 days (longer if she wishes), rescan | Incomplete miscarriage (~80% success) | Unpredictable; only ~50% success in missed miscarriage |
| **Medical** | **Missed**: mifepristone 200 mg then misoprostol 600–800 µg 24–48 h later ⚑ (mifepristone pre-treatment is now standard — MifeMiso). **Incomplete**: misoprostol 600–800 µg alone, repeatable | Avoiding theatre | Cramping, bleeding, diarrhoea, fever/chills; ~5–15% need surgery |
| **Surgical** | **Suction aspiration** with prophylactic **doxycycline ± metronidazole** | Heavy bleeding, sepsis, instability, molar suspicion, choice | Perforation, infection, **Asherman syndrome** — **avoid sharp curettage** ⚑ |

**Aftercare**: histopathology (excludes molar disease), anti-D per above, immediate contraception if wanted, and explicit acknowledgement of grief. Fertility returns immediately — no evidence-based reason to delay conceiving.

**Recurrent miscarriage** — RANZCOG defines it as **two or more** losses <20 weeks, consecutive or not ⚑ (older texts say three).
- **Investigate**: **lupus anticoagulant and anticardiolipin antibodies** (antiphospholipid syndrome — the only reliably treatable cause), TSH ± TPO antibodies, **3D pelvic ultrasound** for uterine anomaly.
- **Case-by-case**: parental karyotype, ANA, sperm DNA fragmentation.
- **Not routine**: inherited thrombophilia screen, NK cell/HLA/cytokine testing, vitamin D, prolactin, homocysteine, routine karyotyping of products.
- **Treat APS** with **low-dose aspirin + prophylactic LMWH** — live birth rate improves substantially. Otherwise supportive care in a dedicated early pregnancy clinic materially improves outcomes; ~75% of women with unexplained recurrent loss go on to a live birth.

**The short version**
- Open os = inevitable/incomplete; closed os with no FH = missed.
- Mifepristone before misoprostol is now standard for missed miscarriage. ⚑
- Recurrent = **two** losses in Australia; the must-do test is antiphospholipid antibodies.

---

## 5.3 Ectopic pregnancy

**1–2% of pregnancies; ~95% tubal (ampulla commonest).** Remains a cause of maternal death — the fatal error is anchoring on "miscarriage".

**Risk factors** (all reduce tubal function or bypass it): previous ectopic (10–15% recurrence), previous PID/chlamydia, tubal surgery including **sterilisation and reversal**, **IUD in situ** (reduces absolute risk of any pregnancy, but a pregnancy that occurs is proportionally more likely ectopic), **IVF/ART**, endometriosis, smoking, progestogen-only contraception, increasing age. **Up to a third have no risk factors** — never exclude on risk profile.

**Presentation**: 6–8 weeks' amenorrhoea, **unilateral lower abdominal pain preceding light dark "prune juice" bleeding**, cervical excitation, adnexal tenderness, uterus small for dates. Ruptured: sudden severe pain, **shoulder-tip pain** (diaphragmatic irritation), syncope, shock, peritonism. Atypical: diarrhoea, urge to defecate, dizziness. **Any woman of reproductive age with abdominal pain and collapse gets a pregnancy test.**

**Investigation**
- **Urine/serum hCG** then **transvaginal ultrasound** — the definitive test. Look for an **adnexal mass separate from the ovary** ("bagel"/"blob" sign), free fluid, empty uterus. A **pseudo-sac** (central, no double decidual ring) is not a pregnancy.
- **hCG discriminatory zone** — the level above which an intrauterine pregnancy *should* be visible on TVUS. Quoted between **1500 and 3500 IU/L** with **no single international consensus**; higher figures (3500) are used deliberately to avoid interrupting a wanted early IUP. ⚑ In multiple pregnancy the zone is higher. An hCG above the zone with an empty uterus = ectopic until proven otherwise.
- **48-hour serial hCG** in a stable woman with a PUL (below).

**Management** — driven by stability, hCG, mass size and fetal cardiac activity.

| | Criteria | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Expectant** | Stable, minimal/no symptoms, **hCG <1500 IU/L** and falling, mass **<35 mm** with **no fetal heart**, reliable follow-up | Serial hCG to non-pregnant level; safety-net explicitly |
| **Methotrexate** | Stable, no significant pain, mass **<35 mm**, **no fetal cardiac activity**, no haemoperitoneum, normal FBC/LFT/U&E, no breastfeeding, no MTX contraindication, able to attend follow-up | **RANZCOG**: single dose if **hCG <3000 IU/L or mass <20 mm**; **two doses 4–7 days apart** if **hCG 3000–5000 IU/L or mass 20–35 mm** ⚑. Older texts quote a single "hCG <5000" cut-off — a real source difference; the RANZCOG two-tier version is the current Australian answer |
| **Surgery** | Haemodynamic instability, significant pain, **rupture**, hCG high or rising, mass ≥35 mm, **fetal cardiac activity**, MTX contraindicated/failed, unable to follow up | **Laparoscopic salpingectomy** is preferred. **Salpingostomy** only if the contralateral tube is absent/damaged and future fertility is a priority — higher failure and needs hCG surveillance for **persistent trophoblast** |

**Methotrexate practicalities**: 50 mg/m² IM, then **hCG on days 4 and 7** — expect a **≥15% fall from day 4 to day 7**; if not, second dose or surgery. Weekly hCG thereafter until <5 IU/L. Counsel: avoid alcohol, NSAIDs, folate supplements and sun exposure; expect transient "separation pain" around days 2–3 (must be distinguished from rupture); **avoid conception for ~4 months**. Side effects: stomatitis, transaminitis, marrow suppression.

**Non-tubal ectopics**: **interstitial/cornual** (presents later, ~8–10 weeks, catastrophic bleeding), **cervical**, **caesarean scar**, **ovarian**, and **heterotopic** — rare spontaneously (~1:30,000) but **~1:100 after IVF**, so a confirmed IUP does *not* exclude an ectopic after ART ⚑.

---

## 5.4 Pregnancy of unknown location (PUL)

**Definition**: positive hCG, **no intrauterine and no extrauterine pregnancy visible** on TVUS. It is a *state*, not a diagnosis — the three destinations are early IUP, failing/failed pregnancy, and occult ectopic. Roughly 7–20% of PULs turn out to be ectopic.

**Serial hCG at 0 and 48 h**:

| hCG behaviour over 48 h | Interpretation | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **Rise ≥63–66%** | Likely viable IUP (but **ectopic not excluded**) | Rescan in ~7–10 days / when hCG >discriminatory zone |
| **Fall ≥50%** | Likely failing pregnancy | Urine hCG at 2 weeks to confirm negative |
| **Suboptimal rise (<63%) or slow fall (<50%) — the "plateau"** | **Highest ectopic risk** | Senior review, close surveillance, low threshold for laparoscopy |

Doubling time in a normal IUP is ~1.4–2.1 days. Progesterone <20 nmol/L supports a failing pregnancy and >60 nmol/L a viable one, but neither **localises** the pregnancy. **Manage clinically, not numerically** — pain and haemodynamic status override any hCG trend. Every PUL leaves with written safety-net advice and a named follow-up point.

---

## 5.5 Hyperemesis gravidarum

**Nausea and vomiting of pregnancy (NVP)** affects up to 80%; **HG** is the severe end. SOMANZ definition: pregnancy-caused nausea/vomiting with significant reduction in oral intake and **weight loss of at least 5%** of pre-pregnancy weight (typically with dehydration and electrolyte or ketotic disturbance). Onset **before 9 weeks** — new-onset vomiting after ~10–11 weeks should prompt a search for another cause (UTI, gastroenteritis, hepatitis, pancreatitis, appendicitis, thyrotoxicosis, raised ICP, DKA).

**Assess with PUQE-24** (nausea hours + vomits + retches): **mild 4–6, moderate 7–12, severe ≥13**. Check weight, ketones, U&E (hypokalaemia, hypochloraemic metabolic alkalosis), LFTs, TFTs, urine MCS, and **ultrasound — exclude multiple pregnancy and molar pregnancy**, both of which raise hCG and drive vomiting.

**Treatment ladder (SOMANZ)**
1. **Non-pharmacological**: small frequent dry carbohydrate meals, avoid triggers, **ginger up to 1200 mg/day** or **pyridoxine (B6) up to 200 mg/day**.
2. **First-line drugs**: antihistamine/dopamine antagonists — **doxylamine (often combined with pyridoxine)**, metoclopramide, prochlorperazine.
3. **Second-line**: **ondansetron 4–8 mg TDS**. Early registry signals of a small absolute increase in **cleft palate** with first-trimester exposure have not been confirmed in updated pooled analyses ⚑ — the current position is no significant increase in major malformations, but document a risk–benefit discussion for T1 use.
4. **Third-line/refractory**: corticosteroids (prednisolone 40–50 mg/day or hydrocortisone 100 mg IV BD), acid suppression (ranitidine alternatives/PPI), and consideration of enteral nutrition.

**Fluids and the Wernicke trap — this is the exam point.**
