- Rehydrate with **normal saline** (1–2 L, ~1 L/h initially), with potassium replacement as needed. **Avoid dextrose-containing fluids until thiamine has been given** — a glucose load in a thiamine-depleted woman can precipitate **Wernicke's encephalopathy**.
- **Give thiamine to any woman vomiting >3 weeks or requiring IV rehydration** — oral 100 mg daily, or IV/IM thiamine (commonly 100 mg IV daily for 3 days, higher doses if Wernicke's is suspected) **before** any glucose.
- **Wernicke's triad**: confusion, ophthalmoplegia/nystagmus, ataxia — often incomplete; a low threshold to treat.
- Also avoid over-rapid correction of hyponatraemia (**central pontine myelinolysis**) and watch for **refeeding syndrome** (hypophosphataemia) when nutrition restarts.

**Also remember**: **VTE prophylaxis** (dehydration + immobility), dental care (acid erosion), and mental health — HG carries substantial psychological morbidity and is a recognised reason women terminate wanted pregnancies. Recurrence is common (~70–80%); pre-emptive treatment before symptom onset helps.

---

## 5.6 Gestational trophoblastic disease

A spectrum from benign molar pregnancy to malignant **gestational trophoblastic neoplasia (GTN)**. Risk factors: **extremes of maternal age (<16, >45)**, previous molar pregnancy, Asian ethnicity.

| | **Complete mole** | **Partial mole** |
|---|---|---|
| Genetics | **Diploid, entirely paternal (46,XX ~75–80%)** — empty ovum fertilised by one sperm that duplicates, or two sperm | **Triploid (69,XXX / 69,XXY)** — normal ovum + two sperm sets |
| Fetal tissue | **Absent** | **Present** (often abnormal fetus/fetal red cells) |
| Villi | Diffuse hydropic swelling, circumferential trophoblast proliferation | Focal, scalloped villi |
| **p57 immunostain** | **Negative** (no maternal genome) | **Positive** |
| hCG | Very high | Modest/normal |
| Progression to GTN | **~15–25%** | **~0.5–4%** |

**Presentation**: first-trimester bleeding (often with passage of "grape-like" vesicles), **uterus large for dates**, **hCG far higher than expected**, **exaggerated pregnancy symptoms — hyperemesis**, theca lutein cysts, and the classics that appear *before 20 weeks*: **early-onset pre-eclampsia** and **hyperthyroidism** (hCG cross-reacts at the TSH receptor). Ultrasound: **"snowstorm"/cluster-of-grapes** heterogeneous mass with cystic spaces, no fetal parts (complete). Many are now diagnosed histologically after an apparently ordinary miscarriage — **which is why all products of conception go to histopathology**.

**Management**: **suction evacuation** under ultrasound guidance — **avoid medical management, and avoid oxytocin until evacuation is under way** (theoretical trophoblast embolisation). Anti-D if Rh D negative. Send tissue for histology **with p57 immunostaining** and register with a GTD centre where available.

**Surveillance**: **partial mole** — weekly hCG until **three consecutive normal results**, then stop. **Complete mole** — hCG to normal, then **monthly for 6 months**. Avoid pregnancy until surveillance is complete; **COCP is acceptable once hCG has normalised** (avoid an IUD until then).

**GTN** — defined by hCG behaviour, not histology alone: **plateau across four values over 3 weeks**, **rise >10% across three values over 2 weeks**, persistent elevation ≥6 months, histological choriocarcinoma, or metastases (**lung commonest** — haemoptysis or a new lung lesion after a molar pregnancy). The malignant group is invasive mole, choriocarcinoma, placental site and epithelioid trophoblastic tumour. FIGO/WHO scoring: **low risk → single-agent methotrexate (with folinic acid) or actinomycin D; high risk → multi-agent EMA-CO**. Cure rates are excellent and fertility is usually preserved.

**The short version**
- Complete = no fetus, all-paternal diploid, p57 negative, 15–25% → GTN.
- Large-for-dates uterus + hyperemesis + pre-eclampsia before 20 weeks = mole.
- GTN is defined by an hCG plateau or rise, not by symptoms.

---

## 5.7 Termination of pregnancy — Australian legal framework in brief

**Abortion is decriminalised in every Australian state and territory.** ⚑ (Queensland 2018, NSW 2019, SA 2021, WA's reform 2023 — the older "unlawful procurement" framing in textbooks is obsolete.) What varies is the **gestational limit for termination on request** and what is required beyond it.

| Jurisdiction | On request up to | Beyond that limit |
|---|---|---|
| ACT | **No gestational limit** | — |
| NSW | **22 weeks** | Two specialist medical practitioners |
| NT | 24 weeks | Two doctors (one must assess the woman) |
| Qld | **22 weeks** | Two doctors |
| SA | 22 weeks 6 days | Two doctors (risk to life/health or serious fetal anomaly) |
| Tas | **16 weeks** (most restrictive) | Two doctors, one an O&G specialist |
| Vic | **24 weeks** | Two doctors |
| WA | 23 weeks | Two doctors |

Common to all jurisdictions: **informed consent**; **counselling offered, not mandated**; **safe access zones** (typically 150 m); and a **conscientious objection** provision permitting refusal to perform the procedure but imposing a **duty to declare the objection promptly and transfer care or name a non-objecting practitioner**, with an overriding obligation to act in an **emergency**. Exam framing: a doctor may decline to perform a termination but may **not** obstruct access or withhold the information needed to obtain one.

**Methods**
- **Medical (MS-2 Step)** — **mifepristone 200 mg** then **misoprostol 800 µg** 36–48 h later, approved to **63 days (9 weeks)**. PBS-listed and, since 2023, prescribable by qualified nurse practitioners and endorsed midwives without the old certification scheme ⚑ — a substantial widening of rural access. Confirm completion at ~2 weeks.
- **Surgical** — suction aspiration to ~12–14 weeks; **dilatation and evacuation** with cervical preparation beyond.
- **Always**: exclude ectopic before medical abortion, **anti-D if Rh D negative**, screen/treat chlamydia, and start contraception immediately — fertility returns within ~2 weeks.

---

## Rapid-Recall Discriminators — Section 5

| Clue / question | Answer |
|---|---|
| MSD and CRL cut-offs that define a failed pregnancy | **MSD ≥25 mm with no embryo**; **CRL ≥7 mm with no fetal heart** |
| Bleeding, cramping, **open** cervical os | **Inevitable** (or incomplete if tissue retained) — os status decides |
| Empty uterus on scan, no previously confirmed IUP | **Not** complete miscarriage — assume **ectopic/PUL** until excluded |
| Bradycardia + hypotension with products at the os | **Cervical shock** — remove tissue with sponge forceps |
| Medical management of **missed** miscarriage | **Mifepristone 200 mg → misoprostol 600–800 µg 24–48 h later** ⚑ |
| Why suction aspiration rather than sharp curettage | Avoids intrauterine adhesions (**Asherman**) |
| Recurrent miscarriage — Australian definition and key test | **≥2 losses <20 weeks**; **antiphospholipid antibodies** (LA + aCL) |
| Treatment for recurrent miscarriage due to APS | **Aspirin + prophylactic LMWH** |
| Commonest site of ectopic | Tubal **ampulla** (~95% tubal overall) |
| hCG discriminatory zone | **~1500–3500 IU/L** — no single consensus; above it, an empty uterus means ectopic until excluded ⚑ |
| Amenorrhoea, unilateral pain **before** light dark bleeding, cervical excitation | **Ectopic** — pain precedes bleeding (miscarriage is the reverse) |
| Shoulder-tip pain + syncope in early pregnancy | **Ruptured ectopic** with haemoperitoneum |
| Expectant management criteria for ectopic | Stable, **hCG <1500 and falling**, mass **<35 mm**, no fetal heart |
| RANZCOG methotrexate dosing split | **Single dose** if hCG <3000 or mass <20 mm; **two doses 4–7 days apart** if hCG 3000–5000 or mass 20–35 mm ⚑ |
| Methotrexate monitoring | hCG **days 4 and 7** — need a **≥15% fall**; then weekly to <5 IU/L |
| Absolute indication for surgery over methotrexate | **Fetal cardiac activity**, rupture, instability, hCG too high, unreliable follow-up |
| Salpingectomy or salpingostomy? | **Salpingectomy** unless the other tube is compromised |
| Confirmed IUP after IVF plus persisting pain and free fluid | **Heterotopic pregnancy** (~1:100 after ART) ⚑ |
| PUL hCG rise ≥63–66% in 48 h | Probable IUP — but **does not exclude ectopic**; rescan |
| PUL hCG that plateaus (<63% rise, <50% fall) | **Highest ectopic risk** — senior review |
| PUQE score defining severe NVP | **≥13** (mild 4–6, moderate 7–12) |
| Weight-loss criterion for hyperemesis gravidarum | **≥5%** of pre-pregnancy weight |
| Fluid to avoid before thiamine in HG | **Dextrose** — precipitates **Wernicke's encephalopathy** |
| Wernicke's triad | Confusion + **ophthalmoplegia/nystagmus** + ataxia |
| Second-line antiemetic in HG and its controversy | **Ondansetron** — early cleft-palate signal not confirmed on updated pooling ⚑ |
| Vomiting starting at 14 weeks with no prior nausea | **Not HG** — look for another cause |
| Karyotype and p57 of a complete mole | **Diploid, wholly paternal (46,XX)**; **p57 negative** |
| Pre-eclampsia at 16 weeks | **Molar pregnancy** until excluded |
| hCG surveillance after complete vs partial mole | Complete: **monthly for 6 months** after normalisation. Partial: weekly to **3 normal results** |
| Definition of GTN on hCG | **Plateau over 4 values/3 weeks**, or **rise >10% over 3 values/2 weeks** |
| Commonest metastatic site for choriocarcinoma | **Lung** |
| Chemotherapy for low-risk vs high-risk GTN | **Single-agent methotrexate/actinomycin D** vs **EMA-CO** |
| Australian state with the lowest on-request gestational limit | **Tasmania (16 weeks)**; ACT has **no limit** |
| Duty of a conscientious objector to termination in Australia | Declare the objection promptly and **transfer care / provide details of a non-objecting provider**; must still act in an emergency |
| Early medical abortion regimen and gestational limit | **Mifepristone 200 mg → misoprostol 800 µg**, up to **63 days** |

### Sources
- RANZCOG. *Miscarriage, Recurrent Miscarriage and Ectopic Pregnancy* (C-Gyn 38).
- Queensland Clinical Guidelines. *Early pregnancy loss* (MN.011) and the accompanying ectopic pregnancy flowchart; Queensland Health.
- RANZCOG. *Management of Gestational Trophoblastic Disease*; cross-checked against FIGO (Ngan HYS et al., *Diagnosis and management of gestational trophoblastic disease*, **Int J Gynecol Obstet**) and RCOG Green-top 38.
- SOMANZ. *Guideline for the Management of Nausea and Vomiting in Pregnancy and Hyperemesis Gravidarum*, 2020; cross-checked against RCOG Green-top 69 (2024) and NSW Health GL2022_009.
- MSI Australia / Children by Choice summaries of state and territory termination-of-pregnancy legislation: Abortion Law Reform Act 2019 (NSW), Termination of Pregnancy Act 2018 (Qld), Termination of Pregnancy Act 2021 (SA), Abortion Law Reform Act 2008 (Vic), Reproductive Health (Access to Terminations) Act 2013 (Tas), Health Act 1993 (ACT), Termination of Pregnancy Law Reform Act 2017 (NT), Abortion Legislation Reform Act 2023 (WA).
- Therapeutic Guidelines (eTG) and Australian Prescriber — early pregnancy and medical abortion topics.

*Cross-references: Section 4.4 (magnesium, antihypertensives) for eclampsia arising from molar pregnancy; Section 4.9 for hyperemesis-associated thiamine deficiency alongside ASM counselling; contraception detail sits in the gynaecology sections.*


---

# O&G Notes — Section 6: Antepartum haemorrhage, placental problems and preterm birth
*Anchored to Queensland Clinical Guidelines (PPROM, preterm labour, antenatal corticosteroids, PPH), RANZCOG (Vasa Praevia C-Obs 47, Placenta Accreta Spectrum C-Obs 20), WA KEMH and SA Perinatal Practice Guidelines, and the National Blood Authority Rh D guideline. Scope: bleeding after 20 weeks, abnormal placentation, and the preterm birth package. AU practice throughout; divergence from NICE/RCOG flagged inline.*

---

## 6.1 Antepartum haemorrhage — the framework

APH = genital tract bleeding from **20+0 weeks** to birth. ⚑ The 20-week floor is the AU convention (matching the stillbirth definition); UK texts often say 24 weeks.

| Grade (Safer Care Victoria) | Loss |
|---|---|
| Spotting | Staining/streaking on pad |
| Minor | <50 mL |
| Major | 50–1000 mL, **no shock** |
| Massive | >1000 mL **and/or shock** |

Two traps. **Visible loss underestimates true loss in abruption** — much can be concealed retroplacentally, so a woman can be shocked with a dry pad. And **the fetus decompensates before the mother**: uteroplacental flow is sacrificed to defend maternal BP, so an abnormal CTG may be the first sign while obs still look reassuring.

Causes: praevia, abruption, vasa praevia, uterine rupture, marginal sinus bleed, and local causes (ectropion, cervicitis, polyp, cervical cancer, trauma, "show"). About half is unexplained — but unexplained APH still predicts SGA and preterm birth, so it earns growth surveillance, not reassurance.

**The rule that gets examined most: no digital vaginal examination until the placental site is known.** Speculum is permitted and useful (visualises cervix, quantifies loss, excludes local causes, checks dilatation and membranes). A digit through a praevia can provoke catastrophic haemorrhage. Both WA KEMH and Safer Care Victoria state this explicitly.

**Investigations:** FBC, group and hold/crossmatch, coagulation profile and **fibrinogen** (falls early in abruption-related DIC — the single most useful marker), U&Es, **Kleihauer if Rh D negative**, CTG once viable, ultrasound for placental site.

**Anti-D — Australian doses (National Blood Authority 2024):** **250 IU** for a sensitising event ≤12 weeks; **625 IU** from 13 weeks (including APH, trauma, ECV); **625 IU routine prophylaxis at 28 and 34 weeks**; **625 IU postnatally** if the baby is Rh D positive. Give within **72 h** (up to 10 days, reduced efficacy). After 20 weeks quantify FMH and add **100 IU per mL of fetal red cells above 6 mL**. ⚑ AU doses are in IU (625 IU ≈ 125 µg), not the UK's 500/1500 IU.

**The short version**
- APH = bleeding ≥20+0 weeks; visible loss underestimates abruption.
- **No digital VE until placental site known** — speculum is fine.
- Fibrinogen is the early DIC marker; Kleihauer if Rh D negative.
- AU anti-D: **250 IU ≤12 weeks, 625 IU thereafter and at 28/34 weeks.**

---

## 6.2 Praevia vs abruption vs vasa praevia — the discriminators

| Feature | **Placenta praevia** | **Placental abruption** | **Vasa praevia** | Uterine rupture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Pain | **Painless** | **Painful** — constant, severe | Painless | Sudden severe pain, may ease after rupture |
| Uterus | **Soft, non-tender** | **Woody-hard, tender, irritable**, hypertonic | Soft | Contractions cease; **fetal parts palpable abdominally** |
| Bleeding | Bright red, **revealed**, recurrent, escalating | Dark, **may be concealed** — loss ≠ severity | Modest volume, **at membrane rupture** | Variable, may be intra-abdominal |
| Mother | Shock **proportional** to visible loss | Shock **out of proportion**; DIC | **Mother is well** — the blood is fetal | Shock, haematuria, shoulder tip pain |
| Fetus | Usually well | Distressed or dead | **Catastrophic** — bradycardia/sinusoidal then death | Acute bradycardia |
| Presentation | **High head, malpresentation** (placenta occupies lower segment) | Engaged, normal lie | Normal | Presenting part recedes |
| Discriminator | **Painless + soft uterus + abnormal lie** | **Painful + woody uterus + fetal compromise** | **Fetal compromise, well mother, triggered by ARM/SROM** | **Prior scar + loss of contractions + station regression** |

Hook: **praevia bleeds the mother, vasa praevia bleeds the baby, abruption bleeds into the uterus.**

**Placenta praevia.** ⚑ **Grades I–IV (and marginal/partial/complete) are abandoned.** Current terminology is binary, defined on **transvaginal ultrasound** (safe in praevia, far more accurate than transabdominal): **placenta praevia** = covers the internal os; **low-lying placenta** = edge **<20 mm** from the os but not covering. Most low-lying placentas at the 18–20 week morphology scan resolve as the lower segment forms — **rescan ~32 weeks**, again at ~36 weeks if still low. **An edge <20 mm from the os in the third trimester generally means caesarean**; ≥20 mm can usually attempt vaginal birth.
Risks: **previous caesarean** (dose-dependent), previous praevia, prior uterine surgery/curettage, multiparity, multiple pregnancy, advanced maternal age, smoking, ART.
Management: avoid intercourse and digital VE; anti-D; corticosteroids if preterm birth anticipated; inpatient care for recurrent or major bleeds; correct anaemia antenatally. Planned caesarean **around 36–38 weeks, individualised** (earlier for recurrent bleeding; Safer Care Victoria advises against elective birth before 38 weeks if truly asymptomatic). Praevia is a major PPH risk — the lower segment contracts poorly, so the "living ligature" fails.

**Placental abruption.** Incidence ~0.4–1%; recurrence 4–12%. Strongest risk factor is **previous abruption**; then **hypertension/pre-eclampsia**, abdominal trauma (**consider intimate partner violence**), **smoking, cocaine/methamphetamine**, PPROM, chorioamnionitis, **sudden uterine decompression** (polyhydramnios, twins), thrombophilia, advanced maternal age, FGR.
⚑ **Abruption is a clinical diagnosis; ultrasound is insensitive** — fresh retroplacental clot is isoechoic with placenta. A visible haematoma rules it *in*; a normal scan excludes nothing. Favourite MCQ distractor.
Complications: **DIC**, shock, AKI, **Couvelaire uterus** (blood tracking into myometrium — bruised, boggy, poorly contractile), **PPH**, fetal hypoxia, stillbirth.
Management: resuscitate, continuous CTG, correct coagulopathy, anti-D. **Fetus alive and compromised → immediate caesarean. Fetus dead → aim for vaginal birth** (amniotomy + oxytocin), because operating into established DIC is dangerous. Expectant management only if preterm, stable, minor and non-progressive.

**Vasa praevia.** Unprotected fetal vessels in the membranes **within 2 cm of the internal os**. Types (RANZCOG C-Obs 47): **1** velamentous cord insertion; **2** vessels between lobes of a bilobed/succenturiate placenta; **3** aberrant marginal vessels, often after a "resolved" praevia. Risks: velamentous insertion, bilobed/succenturiate placenta, low-lying placenta or resolved praevia, **IVF/ART**, multiple pregnancy.
Vignette: **SROM/ARM → modest fresh bleeding → immediate profound fetal bradycardia or sinusoidal CTG, mother entirely well.** Undiagnosed perinatal mortality **44–70%** — a fetus has only ~80–100 mL/kg blood volume, so a trivial-looking loss is exsanguinating.
Detection: TVUS with colour Doppler; RANZCOG suggests assessing cord insertion at the morphology scan, with **TVUS + Doppler at 28–32 weeks** if risk features. Management: corticosteroids, consider admission from ~32 weeks, **planned caesarean from 36+0 weeks** (**34+0** with additional preterm risk factors), before labour and before membranes rupture. Any bleeding or labour = emergency caesarean. ⚑ Apt test/Kleihauer on vaginal blood is obsolete — there is no time.

**The short version**
- **Painless + soft uterus + malpresentation** = praevia. **Painful + woody uterus + fetal distress** = abruption. **Bleeding at SROM + fetal collapse + well mother** = vasa praevia.
- ⚑ Praevia now defined on TVUS: covers os = praevia, <20 mm = low-lying; grades I–IV gone.
- **A normal ultrasound does not exclude abruption.**
- Vasa praevia: planned caesarean **36+0** weeks (34+0 with risk factors).

---

## 6.3 Placenta accreta spectrum (PAS)

Abnormally adherent/invasive placentation from deficient decidua basalis, usually over a caesarean scar.

| Term | Depth |
|---|---|
| **Accreta** | Villi adherent to myometrium; will not separate cleanly |
| **Increta** | Villi **invade into** myometrium |
| **Percreta** | Villi **penetrate through** serosa ± adjacent organs (bladder) |

Independent risks in AU/NZ data (C-Obs 20): **previous caesarean**, **placenta praevia**, older maternal age, multiparity — risk climbs steeply with each additional caesarean. **Praevia + previous caesarean is the accreta stem until proven otherwise.** Also prior myomectomy, curettage, endometrial ablation, Asherman, ART.

Diagnosis: **ultrasound first-line** — loss of the retroplacental clear zone, myometrial thinning, placental lacunae ("moth-eaten"/"Swiss cheese"), bridging vessels on Doppler, bladder wall interruption. **MRI is adjunctive only** (uncertain ultrasound, posterior placenta); it does not outperform ultrasound.

Management: **MDT in a tertiary centre** with massive transfusion, critical care and complex pelvic surgical capability. Planned **caesarean, usually with hysterectomy, leaving the placenta in situ** — RANZCOG explicitly discourages manual removal, and balloon/compression sutures have a role only after spontaneous separation. Uterus-conserving approaches require intensive surveillance and the ability to convert to emergency hysterectomy. Timing is individualised against fetal maturity — most units plan **34–36 weeks** with steroid cover, before labour supervenes; RANZCOG declines to specify a week.

**The short version**
- **Praevia + previous caesarean = suspect accreta.** Ultrasound first-line, MRI only if uncertain.
- Planned caesarean hysterectomy, tertiary MDT; **leave the placenta in situ, do not pull.**

---

## 6.4 Preterm labour

Preterm = birth 20+0 to <37+0 weeks. Subcategories: extremely preterm <28, very preterm 28+0–31+6, moderately preterm 32+0–33+6, late preterm 34+0–36+6. Two-thirds are spontaneous; the rest provider-initiated.

Risks: **previous spontaneous preterm birth** (strongest), short cervix, multiple pregnancy, uterine anomaly, prior cervical surgery (LLETZ/cone), PPROM, infection (BV, UTI, chorioamnionitis, periodontal), APH, polyhydramnios, smoking, low BMI, short inter-pregnancy interval, socioeconomic disadvantage.

**Prediction (QCG).** **Transvaginal cervical length** — screen 16–24 weeks; **<25 mm = short**, with risk rising steeply as length falls. **Fetal fibronectin / Actim Partus** — valid in symptomatic women with intact membranes ~22–36 weeks; **its value is the negative predictive value, 95–98% for ruling out birth within 7 days.** A negative avoids admission, steroids and transfer; a positive is only weakly predictive. Frame it as a **rule-out** test, never rule-in.

**Prevention:** **vaginal progesterone 200 mg nocte, started 16–24 weeks, continued to 36+0** for previous spontaneous preterm birth and/or short cervix. **Cervical cerclage** where length ≤25 mm before 24 weeks with supporting history; history-indicated cerclage for recurrent mid-trimester loss.

### Tocolysis
**Tocolysis buys ~48 hours — enough to complete antenatal corticosteroids and transfer to a centre with neonatal facilities.** It does not improve perinatal mortality on its own; do not tocolyse simply to prolong pregnancy.
- **Agent of choice in Australia: nifedipine (immediate-release).** QCG: 20 mg oral stat, repeatable every 30 min up to three doses, then 20 mg every 3–8 h for up to 48 h (max 160 mg/day). ⚑ A shift from older texts teaching **betamimetics (salbutamol/terbutaline) first-line** — now second-line at best (maternal tachycardia, hyperglycaemia, pulmonary oedema). Atosiban is used in some units; NICE prefers nifedipine with atosiban as alternative.
- **Indomethacin** restricted to **<28–32 weeks**, short courses (ductal constriction, oligohydramnios).
- Contraindications: chorioamnionitis/sepsis, significant APH or abruption, severe pre-eclampsia, non-reassuring fetal status, fetal death, lethal anomaly, gestation beyond ~34 weeks. Nifedipine specifically: maternal hypotension, cardiac disease. **Avoid casually combining nifedipine with MgSO4** — additive hypotension and neuromuscular blockade.

### Antenatal corticosteroids
Reduce **RDS, IVH, NEC and neonatal death** — the highest-impact intervention in preterm birth.
- **Betamethasone 11.4 mg IM × 2 doses, 24 h apart** (may shorten to 12 h if birth likely within 24 h), **or dexamethasone 6 mg IM × 4 doses, 12 h apart.**
- **Window: 22+0 to 34+6 weeks** (QCG) when birth is anticipated within 7 days; at 22+0–23+6 only where active neonatal care is planned. ⚑ The lower bound has crept down — older texts say 24–34 weeks.
- **Maximal benefit if birth occurs 24 h to 7 days after the second dose**, but give even if birth is imminent — a single dose helps.
- **Repeat/rescue:** single further 11.4 mg dose if >7 days since the last with ongoing risk (most effective ≤32+6); QCG caps at three repeat doses.
- **Late preterm:** consider a course 48 h before **planned caesarean >34+6 weeks** with suspected lung immaturity or other risk factors — though deferring elective caesarean to ≥39+0 weeks is usually the better answer.
- Cautions, not contraindications: **diabetes** (expect BGL destabilisation — monitor, often insulin infusion); FGR (repeat doses → lower birth weight); chorioamnionitis. Steroids do **not** increase maternal infection risk.

### Magnesium sulfate for fetal neuroprotection
Same drug as eclampsia prophylaxis, different indication.
- **Anticipated preterm birth before 30+0 weeks, within the next 24 hours** (QCG; SA specifies 24+0 to <30+0).
- **4 g IV load over 20 min, then 1 g/hour until birth or 24 h**, whichever first. Aim to give **as close to 4 h before birth as possible**; never delay an indicated urgent birth for it.
- Reduces **cerebral palsy and gross motor dysfunction** (~63 women treated to prevent one case).
- Monitor RR, deep tendon reflexes, urine output, conscious state. **Loss of patellar reflexes is the earliest toxicity sign**, then respiratory depression, then cardiac arrest. Antidote **calcium gluconate 1 g IV**. Caution in renal impairment (renally cleared).
- ⚑ **AU/UK difference:** Australia uses **<30 weeks**. NICE offers at **24+0–29+6** and *considers* at **30+0–33+6**. In an Australian paper, answer **30 weeks**.

**The short version**
- fFN/Actim Partus is a **rule-out** test (NPV 95–98% for birth within 7 days); cervix <25 mm = short.
- ⚑ **Nifedipine** is the AU tocolytic; tocolysis buys **48 h for steroids and transfer**, nothing more.
- **Betamethasone 11.4 mg IM × 2, 24 h apart, 22+0–34+6 weeks.**
- **MgSO4 4 g then 1 g/h, before 30+0 weeks, birth expected within 24 h** — prevents cerebral palsy.

---

## 6.5 PPROM

Rupture of membranes before 37+0 weeks and before labour. Complicates 2–3% of pregnancies but causes a third of preterm births.

**Diagnosis: sterile speculum** — pooling in the posterior fornix or leakage on Valsalva/cough is the gold standard. If equivocal: **IGFBP-1 (Actim PROM) or PAMG-1 (AmniSure)**; oligohydramnios on ultrasound is supportive, not diagnostic. **Nitrazine/pH paper and ferning are unreliable** (false positives from blood, semen, urine, vaginitis). Take a low vaginal/perianal GBS swab at the same speculum.

⚑ **Avoid digital vaginal examination in PPROM** — QCG states it plainly: digital VE increases ascending infection and shortens latency.

**Antibiotics — the highest-yield fact here.**
- **Erythromycin 250 mg orally 6-hourly for up to 7 days or until birth** (QCG; azithromycin 1 g single dose is an accepted alternative).
- ⚑ **Amoxicillin–clavulanate (co-amoxiclav) is specifically avoided because of increased neonatal necrotising enterocolitis** (ORACLE I). If a stem offers co-amoxiclav for PPROM, it is the wrong answer.
- ⚑ **AU vs UK:** Queensland *adds* **ampicillin/amoxicillin 2 g IV 6-hourly for 48 h, then amoxicillin 250 mg orally 8-hourly** to complete 7 days (Mercer regimen). **NICE uses erythromycin monotherapy 250 mg qds for 10 days or until established labour.** Two differences: the added beta-lactam, and **7 days (AU) vs 10 days (UK)**. If only one drug is offered, **erythromycin**.
- Antibiotics prolong latency and reduce chorioamnionitis, neonatal infection and surfactant use.

**Also give:** antenatal corticosteroids per the window above (PPROM is not a contraindication), **MgSO4 if <30 weeks with birth expected within 24 h**, and **GBS intrapartum prophylaxis** once labour establishes (QCG: **benzylpenicillin 3 g IV load then 1.8 g every 4 h**, ideally ≥4 h before birth; Queensland uses a **risk-based**, not universal-screening, approach — preterm labour is itself a trigger).

| Gestation | Approach |
|---|---|
| **<34+0** | **Expectant** — prematurity risk exceeds the risk of waiting. Steroids, erythromycin, MgSO4 if <30 weeks, close surveillance. |
| **34+0–37+0** | Evidence "limited and inconsistent" (QCG); individualised. Many AU units await spontaneous labour to ~36–37 weeks, earlier if GBS positive. ⚑ A softening from the older "deliver everyone at 34 weeks." |
| Any | **Deliver regardless** if chorioamnionitis, abruption, cord prolapse, established labour or non-reassuring fetal status. |

**Surveillance:** 4-hourly maternal HR, temperature, vaginal loss, uterine tenderness/activity, plus fetal movements and FHR. **Chorioamnionitis is clinical** — fever plus ≥2 of: maternal tachycardia, **fetal tachycardia** (often earliest), uterine tenderness, offensive discharge, WCC >15 × 10⁹/L, CRP >40 (SA PPG). Treat with **broad-spectrum IV antibiotics and expedited birth at any gestation** — not an indication to tocolyse or wait out a steroid course.

**Prolonged PPROM at early gestation:** pulmonary hypoplasia and limb contractures from prolonged oligohydramnios (especially rupture before 22–24 weeks), cord prolapse, abruption, neonatal sepsis.

**The short version**
- Diagnose on **sterile speculum**; **no digital VE**; pH/ferning unreliable.
- ⚑ **Erythromycin, not co-amoxiclav — co-amoxiclav causes NEC.**
- **<34 weeks → expectant + steroids + erythromycin (+ MgSO4 if <30 weeks).**
- Chorioamnionitis at any gestation → **IV antibiotics and deliver.**

---

## Rapid-Recall Discriminators — Section 6

| Clue / question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Painless bright red bleed, **soft non-tender uterus**, transverse lie at 32 weeks | Placenta praevia |
| Constant pain, **woody-hard tender uterus**, shock out of proportion to visible loss | Placental abruption |
| **ARM → fresh bleeding → immediate fetal bradycardia/sinusoidal CTG, mother well** | Vasa praevia — emergency caesarean |
| APH with placental site not documented — what must you NOT do? | **Digital vaginal examination** (speculum only) |
| Suspected abruption, ultrasound normal | **Ultrasound is insensitive — does not exclude it**; manage clinically |
| Earliest lab marker of abruption-related DIC | **Falling fibrinogen** |
| Placental edge <20 mm from internal os, not covering | **Low-lying placenta** (⚑ grades I–IV abandoned) |
| Placenta praevia + two previous caesareans | **Placenta accreta spectrum** — tertiary MDT, caesarean hysterectomy |
| First-line imaging for PAS | **Ultrasound** (MRI adjunct only) |
| At caesarean for accreta the placenta will not separate | **Leave it in situ — do not attempt manual removal** |
| Planned birth for asymptomatic vasa praevia | **36+0 weeks** (34+0 with preterm risk factors) |
| Antibiotic of choice in PPROM | **Erythromycin 250 mg qid** |
| Antibiotic specifically avoided in PPROM, and why | ⚑ **Amoxicillin–clavulanate — neonatal necrotising enterocolitis** |
| Antenatal corticosteroid regimen and window (AU) | **Betamethasone 11.4 mg IM × 2, 24 h apart; 22+0–34+6 weeks** |
| MgSO4 neuroprotection — gestation, dose, purpose | **<30+0 weeks; 4 g IV over 20 min then 1 g/h; reduces cerebral palsy** |
| Earliest sign of magnesium toxicity / antidote | **Loss of patellar reflexes / calcium gluconate 1 g IV** |
| AU tocolytic of choice, and its only real purpose | ⚑ **Nifedipine; 48 h to complete steroids and transfer** |
| Test with high NPV for preterm birth within 7 days | **Fetal fibronectin / Actim Partus** |
| Rh D negative, APH at 26 weeks — anti-D dose | **625 IU** + Kleihauer |
| Fever + fetal tachycardia + uterine tenderness in PPROM at 28 weeks | Chorioamnionitis → **IV antibiotics and deliver**; do not tocolyse |
| GBS intrapartum prophylaxis regimen (AU) | **Benzylpenicillin 3 g IV load, then 1.8 g q4h** |

### Sources
- Queensland Clinical Guidelines: *Preterm prelabour rupture of membranes (PPROM)*; *Preterm labour and birth*; *Short guide: Antenatal corticosteroids*; *Early onset Group B Streptococcal disease*; *Primary postpartum haemorrhage*.
- RANZCOG: *Vasa Praevia* (C-Obs 47, v3, July 2025); *Placenta Accreta Spectrum* (C-Obs 20).
- SA Health: *Preterm Prelabour Rupture of the Membranes*, SA Perinatal Practice Guideline v10.
- WA King Edward Memorial Hospital: *Antepartum Haemorrhage*; *Corticosteroids: Antenatal Use of*.
- Safer Care Victoria: *Antepartum haemorrhage: assessment and management*.
- National Blood Authority Australia: *Guideline for the prophylactic use of Rh D immunoglobulin in pregnancy care*, 2024.
- NICE NG25 *Preterm labour and birth* — cited only for stated UK/AU divergences.

---
---

# O&G Notes — Section 7: Labour and intrapartum care
*Anchored to Queensland Clinical Guidelines (Normal birth, Prolonged/obstructed labour, Intrapartum fetal surveillance, Induction of labour, Instrumental vaginal birth, Primary PPH), RANZCOG (Intrapartum Fetal Surveillance C-Obs 1, Management of Breech Presentation), the ACSQHC Third and Fourth Degree Perineal Tears Clinical Care Standard, and WA KEMH labour guidelines. CTG is described in words only — no figure required.*

---

## 7.1 Stages of labour and normal progress

**First stage** — onset of labour to full dilatation.
- **Latent phase:** irregular painful contractions with effacement and dilatation **up to 4–6 cm**; may last days. This is not "failure to progress." ⚑ The old teaching that active labour starts at 3–4 cm has shifted — contemporary data (Zhang; WHO Labour Care Guide) show the curve does not steepen until ~5–6 cm, and the **Friedman "1 cm/hour" alert line is obsolete**.
- **Established/active phase:** regular painful contractions with progressive dilatation from at least 4 cm. **Normal progress ≥0.5 cm/hour (2 cm in 4 hours)** — below this with adequate contractions, escalate.

**Second stage** — full dilatation to birth. **Passive** = full dilatation without expulsive effort (allows descent and rotation; valuable with an epidural or OP position). **Active** = expulsive pushing or head visible.

| | Nulliparous | Multiparous |
|---|---|---|
| Birth expected within | **3 hours** | **2 hours** |
| Suspect delay after (active) | **1 hour** | **30 minutes** |
| Escalate as duration approaches | **2 hours** | **1 hour** |

Epidural prolongs the second stage — allow a longer passive phase, add roughly an hour.

**Third stage** — birth of baby to birth of placenta. Prolonged if **>30 min (active management)** or **>60 min (physiological)**.

**Routine care (QCG Normal birth):** VE offered **4-hourly** in active labour; intermittent auscultation **every 15–30 min in the first stage** and **at least every 5 min in the active second stage** (immediately after a contraction, for a full minute); **deferred cord clamping ≥1 minute** or until pulsation ceases.

**The three Ps of poor progress** — Power (inadequate contractions; commonest in nulliparas, correctable with amniotomy and oxytocin), Passenger (malposition, malpresentation, macrosomia, deflexion, caput and moulding), Passage. ⚑ **Beware oxytocin in the multipara with poor progress** — in a parous uterus, failure to progress far more often means obstruction, and augmenting obstruction risks rupture. QCG: use oxytocin cautiously in multiparas, never with signs of obstruction.

**Signs of obstructed labour:** maternal and fetal tachycardia, pyrexia, oedematous vulva/cervix, haematuria, vaginal bleeding, constant severe pain, hypertonic contractions, and **Bandl's ring** (palpable retraction ring between thick upper and thinned lower segment — imminent rupture).

Oxytocin target: **3–4 contractions per 10 min, each 40–60 s, ≥60 s rest between.** More than 5 in 10 = tachysystole.

**The short version**
- Active first stage from ~4–6 cm; **normal progress ≥0.5 cm/h**. ⚑ Friedman's 1 cm/h is obsolete.
- Second stage: birth within **3 h nullip / 2 h multip**; suspect delay after **1 h / 30 min** of pushing.
- Poor progress in a **multipara** = obstruction until proven otherwise.

---

## 7.2 Fetal monitoring and CTG interpretation

**Who gets what.** Intermittent auscultation suits low-risk women in established labour. **Continuous CTG** where risk factors exist: antenatal (pre-eclampsia, diabetes, FGR/SGA, oligo/polyhydramnios, ≥41 weeks, multiple pregnancy, breech, APH, reduced fetal movements, previous caesarean) or intrapartum (oxytocin, epidural, **meconium**, abnormal auscultation, PV bleeding, **maternal pyrexia ≥38 °C**, suspected chorioamnionitis, excessive uterine activity, prolonged labour).

Context first: **CTG is sensitive but poorly specific for fetal acidaemia.** Continuous monitoring in low-risk labour raises caesarean and instrumental rates without reducing cerebral palsy — which is why RANZCOG restricts it to risk-flagged labours. Never interpret a trace without gestation, stage, meconium, temperature, contraction frequency and progress.

### The four features — in words

**Baseline rate** — mean FHR over 5–10 min, excluding accelerations and decelerations. **Normal 110–160 bpm.**
- **Tachycardia >160**: maternal pyrexia/chorioamnionitis (commonest), dehydration, anxiety, beta-agonists, prematurity, fetal anaemia or tachyarrhythmia, and — late and ominously — chronic hypoxia. **A rising baseline through labour is a red flag even while still "normal."**
- **Bradycardia <110**: may be benign at 100–110 post-dates with normal variability; otherwise cord compression, maternal hypotension (post-epidural), rapid descent, hyperstimulation, abruption, cord prolapse, uterine rupture, maternal beta-blockade. **A sustained fall >5 minutes is a bradycardia, not a deceleration — an emergency.**

**Baseline variability** — the bandwidth of beat-to-beat fluctuation; visually, how "fuzzy" versus how "flat" the line is. **The single most important indicator of wellbeing**, because it reflects an intact, well-oxygenated autonomic nervous system. **Normal 6–25 bpm.**
- **Reduced 3–5 bpm** — usually **fetal sleep** (should not exceed ~40 min), also opioids/sedatives, magnesium sulfate, prematurity, fetal tachycardia. Beyond 40 min, or with any other abnormality, it becomes concerning.
- **Absent <3 bpm** — significant; suggests established hypoxia/acidaemia or neurological injury.
- **Increased/saltatory >25 bpm** — a swinging exaggerated pattern associated with acute hypoxia and rapidly evolving compromise. **Do not read it as reassuring "good variability."**
- **Sinusoidal** — a smooth, regular, undulating sine wave, amplitude ~5–15 bpm, **2–5 cycles per minute, with absent beat-to-beat variability and no accelerations**, persisting ≥10–20 min. Pathognomonic of **severe fetal anaemia** — fetomaternal haemorrhage, ruptured vasa praevia, rhesus disease, parvovirus B19. Distinguish from *pseudosinusoidal* (jagged, short-lived, retains variability, often post-opioid), which is benign.

**Accelerations** — rise **≥15 bpm sustained ≥15 seconds** (≥10 bpm/10 s before 32 weeks). Presence effectively excludes acidaemia at that moment; **absence alone with an otherwise normal trace is not pathological**.

**Decelerations** — timing relative to the contraction is everything.

| Type | Shape and timing | Mechanism | Significance |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Early** | Shallow, **uniform, symmetrical**, gradual on and off; **nadir coincides with the contraction peak**; mirrors the contraction | **Head compression** → vagal reflex | **Physiological.** Late first/second stage. No action |
| **Variable** | **Abrupt onset and abrupt recovery** ("V"/"W" shaped), varying shape, depth and timing | **Cord compression** | Usually tolerated. Reassuring if <60 s, <60 bpm deep, quick recovery, normal baseline, retained variability, with "shouldering". **Concerning if:** >60 s, >60 bpm, slow return, **loss of shouldering, overshoot, reduced variability within the deceleration, rising baseline** |
| **Late** | Uniform, gradual, shallow but **repetitive**; begins after the contraction starts, **nadir >20 s after the peak**; recovers after the contraction ends | **Uteroplacental insufficiency** | **Always pathological when repetitive** — the most sinister routine pattern. Escalate |
| **Prolonged** | Fall lasting **>90 s up to 5 min** | Acute event | Urgent assessment; **beyond 5 min it is a bradycardia** — prepare for immediate birth |

Hooks: **VEAL CHOP** (Variable→Cord, Early→Head, Acceleration→OK, Late→Placental). For shape: **early decelerations mirror the contraction; late decelerations lag it; variable decelerations ignore it.**

**Classification.** QCG uses colour-coded escalation (green normal, blue borderline, yellow concerning, red abnormal) rather than the older normal/suspicious/pathological triad. A **normal CTG** = baseline 110–160, variability 6–25, accelerations present, **no decelerations**.

**Intrauterine resuscitation, in order:**
1. **Call for help; stop or halve the oxytocin; remove any prostaglandin pessary.**
2. **Reposition** — left lateral (relieves aortocaval compression); knee–chest if cord compression suspected.
3. **IV fluid bolus**; correct maternal hypotension (ephedrine/metaraminol post-epidural).
4. **Vaginal examination** — exclude **cord prolapse**, assess progress (rapid descent explains many decelerations).
5. **Acute tocolysis** for excessive uterine activity — **terbutaline 250 µg subcutaneously** (or GTN).
6. Treat maternal pyrexia ≥38 °C; screen and treat sepsis.
7. Not resolving → **fetal blood sampling** (if non-urgent and feasible) or **expedite birth**.

⚑ **Routine maternal facemask oxygen is no longer recommended** in a normoxic woman — no demonstrated benefit, possible harm. Older textbooks list it first; it has been dropped.

**Fetal blood sampling (RANZCOG and QCG concur):**

| | pH | Lactate (mmol/L) | Action |
|---|---|---|---|
| Normal | **≥7.25** | **≤4.0** | Continue; repeat in 40–60 min if concerns persist |
| Borderline | **7.21–7.24** | **4.1–4.7** | Repeat in **20–30 min** |
| Abnormal | **≤7.20** | **≥4.8** | **Acidaemia — expedite birth** |

Lactate has largely displaced pH in AU practice (smaller sample, higher success rate). Contraindicated with maternal HIV/hepatitis, suspected fetal bleeding disorder, <34 weeks, and acute compromise where the answer is to deliver. **Fetal scalp stimulation producing an acceleration** is a quicker surrogate for a non-acidaemic fetus.

**The short version**
- **Baseline 110–160; variability 6–25; accelerations ≥15 bpm for ≥15 s.**
- **Early = head = mirrors the contraction = benign. Late = placental = lags the peak by >20 s = pathological. Variable = cord = abrupt V.**
- **Sinusoidal = fetal anaemia; fall >5 min = bradycardia = emergency.**
- Resuscitate: stop oxytocin, left lateral, fluids, exclude cord prolapse, tocolyse. ⚑ **Not routine oxygen.**
- **Lactate ≥4.8 or pH ≤7.20 → deliver.**

---

## 7.3 Malpresentation and malposition

**Malposition** = cephalic but occiput not anterior. **OP** gives severe continuous backache, early urge to push before full dilatation, long latent phase, slow progress, and a deflexed head presenting a larger diameter (occipitofrontal ~11.5 cm vs suboccipitobregmatic 9.5 cm). Most rotate spontaneously — manage with patience, hydration, good analgesia, a longer passive second stage, and oxytocin for inadequate contractions. Persistent OP or **deep transverse arrest** may need rotational instrumental birth (Kielland forceps or rotational vacuum) by an experienced operator, or caesarean.

**Breech** — 3–4% of singletons at term.
- **Frank** (extended legs — commonest, most favourable); **complete** (hips and knees flexed); **footling** (**unsuitable for vaginal birth**, highest cord prolapse risk).
- **ECV (RANZCOG):** offer from **37+0 weeks**, consider from **36+0 in nulliparas** (less spontaneous version after this). Success ~**40% nulliparous, 60% multiparous**. Serious complication needing emergency caesarean ~**1 in 200**; transient FHR changes ~4%. Tocolysis improves success. **Give anti-D 625 IU to Rh D negative women whether ECV succeeds or fails.**
- ECV contraindications: any existing indication for caesarean, **placenta praevia**, APH within 7 days, abnormal CTG, ruptured membranes, multiple pregnancy (except second twin), rhesus isoimmunisation. Relative: FGR with abnormal Dopplers, pre-eclampsia, oligohydramnios, major anomaly, uterine anomaly. **Previous caesarean is not an absolute contraindication.**
- Mode of birth: the **Term Breech Trial (2000)** showed lower perinatal death (0.3% vs 1.3%) and serious neonatal morbidity with planned caesarean, no difference in serious maternal morbidity. ⚑ Later data soften this — a large Dutch cohort suggested ~175 caesareans to avoid one fetal death, and meta-analysis puts mortality ~1/333 vaginal vs 1/2000 caesarean: statistically real, absolutely small. Planned vaginal breech birth is reasonable **with strict selection and a skilled attendant**: frank or complete breech, EFW ~2.5–3.8 kg, **no fetal head hyperextension**, no fetal compromise, continuous CTG, immediate theatre access.

**Transverse/oblique and unstable lie** — think placenta praevia, polyhydramnios, uterine anomaly, high parity, fetal anomaly. **High cord prolapse risk**; admit from ~37 weeks for unstable lie; never labour a persistent transverse lie.

**Brow** presents the mentovertical diameter (~13 cm) — **undeliverable at term, caesarean.** **Face** is deliverable only if **mento-anterior**; **mento-posterior is undeliverable** (the neck cannot extend further).

**Cord prolapse** — risks: malpresentation, unstable lie, polyhydramnios, prematurity, multiple pregnancy, high presenting part, **ARM with an unengaged head** (an iatrogenic cause worth remembering). Management: **call for help; elevate the presenting part manually or by filling the bladder with 500 mL saline; knee–chest or exaggerated Sims; minimal cord handling** (handling provokes vasospasm — keep it warm, do not replace it); tocolysis if needed; **immediate category 1 caesarean** unless vaginal birth is genuinely imminent.

**The short version**
- OP: backache, slow progress, deflexed head — most rotate; be patient.
- ECV from **37+0** (36+0 if nulliparous); **anti-D 625 IU**; success 40/60%.
- **Footling breech, brow and mento-posterior face = caesarean.**
- Cord prolapse: **elevate presenting part, don't handle the cord, category 1 caesarean.**

---

## 7.4 Shoulder dystocia

Birth requiring **additional manoeuvres beyond routine axial traction** after the head delivers — the anterior shoulder impacted behind the symphysis. Clue: the **"turtle sign"** — head delivers then retracts tightly against the perineum, restitution fails.

Risks: macrosomia >4.5 kg, **maternal diabetes** (disproportionate shoulder/abdominal fat), obesity or excessive gestational weight gain, **previous shoulder dystocia** (recurrence 10–15%), post-dates, prolonged second stage, induction/augmentation, **instrumental birth**. ⚑ **Largely unpredictable — around half of cases have none of these risk factors**, and most macrosomic babies deliver uneventfully. Prophylactic induction or caesarean for suspected macrosomia is not routinely justified in a non-diabetic woman.

**HELPERR** (the AU caveat: the order of *internal* manoeuvres matters less than performing them competently and fast):
1. **H — Help.** Obstetrician, senior midwife, anaesthetist, neonatal team. **Note the time the head delivered.**
2. **E — Evaluate for Episiotomy.** Does not relieve a bony impaction, but creates room for the operator's hand.
3. **L — Legs: McRoberts.** Hyperflex and abduct the hips onto the abdomen — flattens the lumbosacral lordosis, rotates the symphysis cephalad. **Resolves ~40–50% alone; always first.**
4. **P — suprapubic Pressure.** From the side of the fetal back, downward and laterally, to adduct the anterior shoulder into the oblique. **McRoberts + suprapubic pressure resolves the large majority.**
5. **E — Enter: internal rotation.** **Rubin II** (pressure on the posterior aspect of the anterior shoulder), **Woods screw** (pressure on the anterior aspect of the posterior shoulder, rotating 180°), reverse Woods screw.
6. **R — Remove the posterior arm.** Sweep it across the chest and deliver — reduces the shoulder-to-shoulder diameter by 2–3 cm. Highly effective; humeral fracture is an acceptable trade.
7. **R — Roll** onto all fours (Gaskin), then reattempt.
8. **Last resort:** cleidotomy, symphysiotomy, **Zavanelli** (replace the head, proceed to caesarean).

**Never:** **fundal pressure** (worsens impaction, risks rupture), excessive downward traction (brachial plexus injury), or twisting/lateral flexion of the neck.

**Complications.** Fetal: **brachial plexus injury — Erb palsy (C5–C6, "waiter's tip")** is classic and >90% resolve (some are pre-existing and unrelated to traction); clavicular/humeral fracture; **HIE** and death if prolonged. Maternal: **PPH (commonest)**, **OASI**, symphyseal separation, psychological sequelae.

**Documentation is examinable:** time of head and body delivery, which shoulder was anterior, sequence and timing of each manoeuvre, who attended and when, cord gases, neonatal condition. Debrief the woman.

**The short version**
- Turtle sign + failed restitution = shoulder dystocia; **half have no risk factors.**
- **McRoberts + suprapubic pressure first.** **Never fundal pressure.**
- Commonest fetal injury **Erb palsy**; commonest maternal complication **PPH**.

---

## 7.5 Induction of labour

**Indications (QCG):** prolonged pregnancy from **41+0 weeks**, PPROM/term PROM, hypertensive disease and pre-eclampsia, diabetes, **intrahepatic cholestasis** (timed by bile acids), FGR/abnormal Dopplers, oligohydramnios, reduced fetal movements, twins from **37+0**, suspected macrosomia from 38+0 with ultrasound, **maternal age ≥40 at 39–40 weeks**, chorioamnionitis, fetal death.

**Contraindications:** anything contraindicating vaginal birth — praevia, vasa praevia, transverse lie, cord prolapse, **active primary genital herpes**, prior **classical** uterine incision or previous rupture, invasive cervical cancer, acute fetal compromise (caesarean instead).

**Modified Bishop score:** cervical **dilatation, length/effacement, consistency, position**, plus **fetal station**. **≥7 = favourable** (ARM ± oxytocin); **≤6 = unfavourable** → ripen first.

| Method | When | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Membrane sweep** | Offer from **39+0 weeks** | Reduces need for formal IOL; best in multiparas; safe in GBS carriers |
| **Balloon catheter** | MBS ≤6 | **Mechanical → lower hyperstimulation risk; preferred with a previous caesarean** |
| **Dinoprostone (PGE2)** gel or 10 mg pessary | MBS ≤6 | Effective; **main risk hyperstimulation** — the pessary can be removed, the gel cannot |
| **ARM** | MBS ≥7 or post-ripening | Needs an engaged head — **ARM with a high head risks cord prolapse** |
| **Oxytocin** | After ARM, or ruptured membranes | **Continuous CTG required**; titrate to 3–4 per 10 min |
| **Misoprostol** | ⚑ Not recommended by QCG for routine live-fetus IOL | Higher odds of hyperstimulation with FHR changes (widely used internationally — know the AU position) |

**Post-term:** offer IOL at **41+0 weeks**; by **42+0** birth is clearly indicated. ⚑ Older UK teaching of "between 41 and 42 weeks" has converged on 41 in AU practice.

**Risks:** **tachystole** = >5 contractions in 10 min averaged over 30 min; **hyperstimulation** = tachysystole or hypertonus **with FHR changes** → **cease/reduce oxytocin, remove the prostaglandin, left lateral, IV fluids, terbutaline or GTN.** Also failed induction, **cord prolapse at ARM**, **uterine rupture** (especially prostaglandins after previous caesarean), more instrumental birth, chorioamnionitis with prolonged rupture.

**VBAC:** balloon preferred for ripening; prostaglandins increase rupture risk (background ~0.5% with spontaneous labour after one lower-segment caesarean, higher with induction/augmentation). **The cardinal sign of scar rupture is acute fetal bradycardia**, often with loss of contractions, station regression, maternal pain and haematuria.

**The short version**
- **Bishop ≥7 → ARM ± oxytocin; ≤6 → ripen** (balloon or dinoprostone).
- ⚑ **Balloon preferred after previous caesarean**; misoprostol not routine in AU.
- **Offer IOL at 41+0 weeks.**
- Hyperstimulation → stop oxytocin, remove pessary, left lateral, fluids, **terbutaline**.

---

## 7.6 Instrumental birth and caesarean section

### Instrumental vaginal birth
**Indications:** delay in the second stage despite adequate contractions; suspected fetal compromise in the second stage; maternal exhaustion; or shortening the second stage where maternal effort is contraindicated (severe cardiac disease, hypertensive crisis, cerebral aneurysm, myasthenia).

**Prerequisites (QCG):** **fully dilated cervix; ruptured membranes; vertex presentation with position known (OA/OT/OP); head ≤1/5 palpable abdominally and at or below the ischial spines; adequate pelvis and EFW; caput and moulding assessed; adequate analgesia; empty bladder; consent; skilled operator; facilities for immediate caesarean if it fails.**

**Station classification:** **outlet** (scalp visible without parting the labia, skull at the pelvic floor, rotation ≤45°); **low** (at/below +2 cm, not at the pelvic floor); **mid-cavity** (head ≤1/5 palpable, at/below the spines but above +2 cm — do these and rotational births in theatre as a **trial**); **high** (head ≥2/5 palpable) — **not appropriate**, except a second twin.

| | **Vacuum** | **Forceps** |
|---|---|---|
| Success | **Higher failure rate** | **More likely to achieve birth** |
| Maternal trauma / OASI | **Lower** (~82/1000) | **Higher** (~150/1000) |
| Cephalohaematoma, retinal haemorrhage, **subgaleal** | **More common** (subgaleal 3–7.6/1000) | Less (subgaleal 1.6/1000) |
| Facial nerve palsy, facial marking, skull fracture | Rare | **More common** |
| Contraindicated | **<34 weeks** (subgaleal/IVH), face presentation, suspected fetal bleeding disorder | Face only if mento-anterior with specific instrument |

Hook: **vacuum spares the mother and risks the baby's scalp; forceps spare the scalp and risk the mother's sphincter.**

**Abandon and convert to caesarean:** vacuum — after **two detachments** (one if inexperienced), no descent on the first three pulls, or **20 minutes**; forceps — blades will not apply without undue force, or no descent after three pulls. **Sequential instrument use increases maternal and neonatal morbidity** — go to caesarean rather than try a second instrument.

**Two things after instrumental birth:** **mediolateral episiotomy** (strongly consider, especially at a first vaginal birth) cut at **≥60° from the fourchette at crowning**; and ⚑ **amoxicillin–clavulanate 1 g + 200 mg IV, single dose within 6 hours** (the ANODE trial) — newer than most textbooks and now in QCG.

### Caesarean section

| Category | Definition | Decision-to-delivery target |
|---|---|---|
| **1** | **Immediate threat to life of woman or fetus** (cord prolapse, prolonged bradycardia, abruption with compromise, scar rupture) | **30 minutes** |
| **2** | Compromise, **not immediately life-threatening** | **60 minutes** |
| **3** | Needs early birth, **no current compromise** | No fixed target |
| **4** | Elective — timing to suit woman and team | ≥39+0 weeks |

⚑ The 30 minutes is a **service audit standard, not a threshold at which harm begins** — deliver as fast as *safely* possible; sacrificing safe anaesthesia for speed is never the right answer.

**Elective caesarean at ≥39+0 weeks** without other indication — earlier birth increases neonatal respiratory morbidity (TTN, RDS). If unavoidable earlier, consider a steroid course 48 h beforehand.

**Peri-operative package:** ⚑ **prophylactic cefazolin before skin incision** (beats post-cord-clamp for maternal infection with no neonatal downside — a change from older teaching); **antacid/aspiration prophylaxis** (famotidine ± metoclopramide, sodium citrate before GA); **regional anaesthesia preferred**; **VTE prophylaxis** (mechanical ± LMWH by risk); **prophylactic oxytocin** (below). Complications: haemorrhage, endometritis and wound infection, VTE, bladder/ureteric/bowel injury; and for future pregnancies **praevia, accreta spectrum, scar rupture, adhesions** — which is why the number of previous caesareans matters.

**The short version**
- **Fully dilated, membranes ruptured, head ≤1/5 palpable and at/below spines, position known, bladder empty, analgesia, consent, theatre available.**
- **Vacuum = more failures and more neonatal scalp injury; forceps = more success and more OASI.**
- **Category 1 = 30 min; category 2 = 60 min; elective ≥39 weeks.**
- ⚑ **Co-amoxiclav single dose after instrumental birth; cefazolin before skin incision at caesarean.**

---

## 7.7 Third stage, perineal trauma and analgesia

### Active management of the third stage
Reduces PPH by 50–60%; recommended for all women with informed choice preserved. Three components: **prophylactic uterotonic, controlled cord traction with counter-traction guarding the uterus, and deferred cord clamping (≥1 min or until pulsation ceases)**. ⚑ **Early cord clamping is no longer part of active management** — deferring improves neonatal haemoglobin and iron stores without increasing PPH; older definitions bundled it in.

| Setting | Prophylactic uterotonic (QCG) |
|---|---|
| Vaginal birth, IV access | **Oxytocin 10 IU IV slowly over 3–5 min** (preferred) |
| Vaginal birth, no IV access | **Oxytocin 10 IU IM** |
| **Caesarean under regional** | **Oxytocin 3–5 IU IV over 1–2 min** with haemodynamic monitoring |

⚑ Note the **lower caesarean dose** — a rapid 10 IU IV bolus causes vasodilatation, hypotension and tachycardia, unsafe with a spinal sympathectomy or cardiac disease. **Carbetocin** is an alternative (heat-stable, long-acting); **misoprostol** only where injectables are unavailable. ⚑ AU first-line prophylaxis is **oxytocin alone**; **Syntometrine** (oxytocin + ergometrine) is reserved for higher-risk women, **contraindicated in hypertension/pre-eclampsia and cardiac disease**, and causes far more nausea and vomiting.

Physiological third stage is a legitimate choice for a low-risk woman — convert to active management for excessive bleeding or if undelivered at one hour.

**Retained placenta** (>30 min active / >60 min physiological): IV access, empty bladder, oxytocin, then **manual removal in theatre under regional anaesthesia** with antibiotic cover. **Do not pull hard on a cord with a fundal placenta** — risk of **uterine inversion** (shock out of proportion to loss, fundal dimple or mass at the introitus; replace immediately, Johnson manoeuvre, and give the uterotonic **only after** replacement).

**PPH (AU definitions):** primary PPH **≥500 mL in 24 h**; **≥1000 mL severe**; **≥2500 mL major haemorrhage**. **Tranexamic acid 1 g IV over 10 min as soon as possible, ideally within 3 hours** (WOMAN trial), repeatable once after 30 min.

### Perineal trauma

| Degree | Definition |
|---|---|
| **First** | Perineal skin and/or vaginal mucosa only |
| **Second** | **Perineal muscles**, sphincter intact (includes episiotomy) |
| **3a** | **<50% of external anal sphincter (EAS)** torn |
| **3b** | **>50% of EAS** torn |
| **3c** | **EAS and internal anal sphincter (IAS)** torn |
| **Fourth** | EAS + IAS + **anorectal mucosa** |

Third and fourth degree together = **OASI**.

**Risks:** first vaginal birth, **South Asian ethnicity**, birth weight >3.5–4 kg, **OP position**, shoulder dystocia, prolonged second stage, **instrumental birth (forceps > vacuum)**, **midline episiotomy**, epidural.

**Prevention:** **warm perineal compresses**, slowing the head at crowning with manual perineal protection, and **mediolateral (not midline) episiotomy at ≥60°** when indicated — midline extends straight into the sphincter, which is why it is not used in Australia.

**Repair:** every woman needs a **systematic perineal and digital rectal examination after birth** — missed OASI is the commonest reason for later incontinence. OASI repair by an **obstetrician, GP obstetrician or colorectal surgeon**, **in theatre** with adequate lighting, anaesthesia and instruments; **repeat the rectal examination after repair** to confirm no suture has penetrated the anorectal mucosa. **Broad-spectrum antibiotics and a stool softener (lactulose, not a bulking agent)**, pelvic floor physiotherapy, structured follow-up, and counselling about mode of birth next pregnancy.

### Analgesia in labour
Non-pharmacological: **continuous one-to-one support has the best evidence base of anything on this list**; also water immersion, TENS, massage, position and mobility.

**Nitrous oxide (Entonox) 50:50** — self-administered, onset in seconds, offset within a minute, no effect on labour progress or the neonate; nausea and light-headedness common.

**Parenteral opioids** — morphine/fentanyl IM or IV, remifentanil PCA in some units. Modest analgesia; **maternal sedation and nausea, reduced CTG variability (a benign cause — know this), delayed gastric emptying, neonatal respiratory depression if given close to birth** (naloxone available).

**Epidural** (low-dose bupivacaine/ropivacaine + fentanyl, catheter at L3/4, infusion or PCEA) — the most effective analgesia in labour.
- **Hypotension** from sympathetic blockade — preload/co-load, vasopressors, left lateral tilt. **Maternal hypotension causes fetal bradycardia** — a common, reversible cause of an abnormal CTG shortly after siting.
- **Maternal fever** — confuses the picture with chorioamnionitis, increasing antibiotics and neonatal septic screens.
- **Urinary retention** — needs catheterisation and bladder care.
- **Longer second stage** and **increased instrumental birth**.
- ⚑ **Epidural does NOT increase the caesarean rate** (Cochrane 2018) — older textbooks and much folk teaching say it does. A very common MCQ discriminator.
- **Post-dural puncture headache** (~1%, from inadvertent dural puncture) — postural, worse sitting up, relieved lying flat; conservative measures then **epidural blood patch**.
- Rare: high/total spinal, LA toxicity, epidural haematoma or abscess, permanent neurological injury (~1 in 100,000).
- **Contraindications:** refusal, coagulopathy or therapeutic anticoagulation, **thrombocytopenia** (generally <70–80 × 10⁹/L), local or systemic sepsis, raised ICP, uncorrected hypovolaemia.
- **Positively indicated** in severe pre-eclampsia (blunts the hypertensive response, if platelets and coagulation permit), cardiac disease, and where operative birth is likely (top-up rather than GA).

**The short version**
- Active third stage = **uterotonic + controlled cord traction + deferred cord clamping ≥1 min**. ⚑ Early clamping is out.
- **Oxytocin 10 IU at vaginal birth; only 3–5 IU IV at caesarean.**
- **3a <50% EAS, 3b >50% EAS, 3c EAS+IAS, 4th adds anorectal mucosa** — always do a rectal exam after birth.
- ⚑ **Epidural increases instrumental birth and maternal fever, but NOT caesarean rate.**

---

## Rapid-Recall Discriminators — Section 7

| Clue / question | Answer |
|---|---|
| Normal progress in established first stage | **≥0.5 cm/hour (2 cm in 4 hours)** |
| Second stage limits, nullip vs multip | Birth within **3 h / 2 h**; suspect delay after **1 h / 30 min** of pushing |
| Multipara with poor progress — the trap | **Suspect obstruction**; be cautious with oxytocin (rupture risk) |
| Palpable ridge between upper and lower segments | **Bandl's ring** — imminent uterine rupture |
| Normal baseline / variability / acceleration | **110–160 bpm / 6–25 bpm / ≥15 bpm for ≥15 s** |
| Deceleration whose nadir coincides with the contraction peak | **Early** — head compression — benign |
| Repetitive deceleration with nadir **>20 s after** the peak | **Late** — uteroplacental insufficiency — pathological |
| Abrupt V-shaped decelerations of varying shape | **Variable** — cord compression |
| Smooth undulating 2–5 cycles/min, absent variability | **Sinusoidal — severe fetal anaemia** (FMH, ruptured vasa praevia, rhesus) |
| FHR fall lasting more than 5 minutes | **Bradycardia — emergency**, prepare for immediate birth |
| Reduced variability shortly after IM morphine | **Opioid effect (or fetal sleep)** — benign if <40 min, otherwise normal |
| Intervention no longer recommended for an abnormal CTG | ⚑ **Routine maternal facemask oxygen** |
| Scalp lactate / pH mandating birth | **Lactate ≥4.8 mmol/L or pH ≤7.20** |
| Head delivers then retracts against the perineum | **Shoulder dystocia** (turtle sign) |
| First manoeuvre in shoulder dystocia | **McRoberts**, then suprapubic pressure |
| Forbidden manoeuvre in shoulder dystocia | **Fundal pressure** |
| Commonest fetal / maternal complication of shoulder dystocia | **Erb palsy (C5–6) / PPH** |
| Bishop score cut-off for a favourable cervix | **≥7** |
| Ripening agent of choice with a previous caesarean | **Balloon catheter** (avoid prostaglandins) |
| Gestation to offer IOL for prolonged pregnancy (AU) | **41+0 weeks** |
| Tachysystole with FHR changes on oxytocin — first actions | **Stop oxytocin, remove pessary, left lateral, fluids, terbutaline 250 µg SC** |
| Acute bradycardia + loss of contractions + station regression in VBAC | **Uterine rupture** |
| Instrument with more OASI vs more neonatal scalp injury | **Forceps / vacuum** |
| Maximum vacuum attempt before abandoning | **3 pulls, 2 detachments, or 20 minutes** |
| ⚑ Drug given routinely after instrumental birth | **Amoxicillin–clavulanate 1 g + 200 mg IV, single dose** |
| Decision-to-delivery targets, category 1 and 2 | **30 minutes / 60 minutes** |
| Earliest gestation for elective caesarean without other indication | **39+0 weeks** |
| Uterotonic dose, vaginal birth vs caesarean | **10 IU IM/slow IV vs 3–5 IU IV** |
| Uterotonic contraindicated in pre-eclampsia | **Ergometrine / Syntometrine** |
| Tear involving >50% of the external anal sphincter | **3b** |
| Episiotomy type and angle used in Australia | **Mediolateral, ≥60° from the fourchette at crowning** |
| ⚑ Outcome epidural does NOT increase | **Caesarean rate** (it does increase instrumental birth and maternal fever) |
| Postural headache after epidural, worse sitting up | **Post-dural puncture headache** → conservative, then **blood patch** |
| PPH definition and TXA dose/window | **≥500 mL in 24 h; TXA 1 g IV over 10 min within 3 hours** |

### Sources
- Queensland Clinical Guidelines: *Normal birth*; *Short guide: Prolonged and/or obstructed labour*; *Intrapartum fetal surveillance (IFS)*; *Induction of labour*; *Short guide: Instrumental vaginal birth*; *Primary postpartum haemorrhage*.
- RANZCOG: *Intrapartum Fetal Surveillance Clinical Guideline* (C-Obs 1) and Summary Booklet; *Management of breech presentation*.
- Australian Commission on Safety and Quality in Health Care: *Third and Fourth Degree Perineal Tears Clinical Care Standard*, 2021.
- WA King Edward Memorial Hospital: *Caesarean Birth*; *Shoulder Dystocia*; *Operative Vaginal Birth*.
- Anim-Somuah M et al. *Epidural versus non-epidural or no analgesia for pain management in labour.* Cochrane Database Syst Rev 2018.
- WOMAN Trial Collaborators, Lancet 2017 (TXA); ANODE trial, Lancet 2019 (antibiotics after instrumental birth); Term Breech Trial, Lancet 2000.


---

# O&G Notes — Section 8: The Postpartum Period
*Anchored to RANZCOG C-Obs 43 (PPH), Queensland Clinical Guidelines (PPH, sepsis), COPE 2023 National Perinatal Mental Health Guideline, RACGP/Therapeutic Guidelines (mastitis), Safer Care Victoria (maternal sepsis). Australian doses and thresholds; UK/NICE divergences flagged inline. Scope: puerperium (birth → 6 weeks), written-finals depth.*

---

## 8.1 Postpartum haemorrhage

The single highest-yield obstetric emergency in written finals. Questions almost always test either **(a) which of the 4 Ts the stem is describing**, or **(b) the next step on the escalation ladder given a specific contraindication**.

**Definitions (Australian).** Primary PPH = blood loss ≥500 mL within 24 h of birth. Severe/major PPH = ≥1000 mL, **or any volume with haemodynamic compromise** — the second half of that clause is the exam point, because a 700 mL loss in a 50 kg anaemic woman who is tachycardic and hypotensive is a major PPH. Massive/critical haemorrhage ≥2500 mL. Secondary PPH = excessive bleeding from **24 h to 6 weeks** postpartum.

⚑ Note the Australian convention: QCG and RANZCOG apply the **same ≥500 mL threshold to both vaginal and caesarean birth**, whereas older textbooks (and much UK teaching) use 500 mL vaginal / 1000 mL caesarean. If a stem gives 800 mL after LSCS, Australian practice calls that a PPH.

**Visual estimation systematically underestimates loss** — weigh swabs, use graduated drapes. Pregnancy-expanded plasma volume means a woman can lose 1500 mL before BP falls: **tachycardia and narrowed pulse pressure precede hypotension**, which is a late and ominous sign.

### The 4 Ts — clue → cause

| T | Share | Clue in the stem | Confirm / treat |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Tone** (atony) | ~70–80% | **Boggy, high, poorly contracted uterus**; bleeding is steady dark loss; prolonged/augmented labour, multiple pregnancy, polyhydramnios, macrosomia, grand multiparity, chorioamnionitis, tocolytics/halogenated anaesthetics | Palpate fundus → rub up a contraction; uterotonic ladder |
| **Trauma** | ~20% | **Well-contracted, firm uterus but continuing bright red bleeding**; instrumental birth, episiotomy, precipitate labour, big baby | Inspect under good light ± lithotomy/theatre; repair. Consider concealed: broad ligament / paravaginal haematoma (pain + shock out of proportion to visible loss), uterine rupture, uterine inversion |
| **Tissue** | ~10% | Placenta incomplete on inspection (**missing cotyledon or ragged membranes**), uterus stays boggy despite uterotonics, previous LSCS/placenta praevia (accreta spectrum) | Explore uterus / manual removal in theatre; USS for retained products |
| **Thrombin** | <1% | Oozing from cannula sites, non-clotting blood, **abruption, severe pre-eclampsia/HELLP, IUFD, amniotic fluid embolism, sepsis, known bleeding disorder or therapeutic anticoagulation** | Coags, fibrinogen, ROTEM; targeted product replacement |

**Hook:** *Firm uterus + bleeding = look for a tear. Boggy uterus + bleeding = squeeze and drug it.* Uterine inversion is the trap — **fundus not palpable abdominally + a mass at the introitus + shock disproportionate to loss**; replace immediately (manual, or hydrostatic O'Sullivan technique) and do **not** give a uterotonic until it is back in.

### Prevention — active management of the third stage

Roughly halves PPH risk. Prophylactic oxytocin: **10 IU IM** (vaginal birth, no IV access), or **10 IU IV slowly over 3–5 min**, or **3–5 IU IV over 1–2 min at caesarean**. Plus controlled cord traction with counter-traction (guarding the uterus), and post-birth fundal assessment. ⚑ Early cord clamping is no longer part of active management — deferred clamping ≥60 s is standard and does not increase PPH.

### The escalation ladder

Run mechanical, pharmacological, and resuscitative arms **in parallel**, not in sequence. Call for help early.

1. **Mechanical / first 60 seconds** — rub up the fundus, **empty the bladder** (a full bladder splints the uterus open), bimanual compression if torrential. Two large-bore cannulae, **FBC, coags, fibrinogen, group & hold/crossmatch**, warmed fluids, oxygen.
2. **Oxytocin 5 IU IV over 1–2 min**, repeatable once (max 10 IU bolus), then infusion **30–40 IU in 500 mL crystalloid at ~5–10 IU/h**. Watch hypotension with rapid bolus and hyponatraemia with large cumulative volumes.
3. **Ergometrine 250–500 microgram IM or slow IV**, repeat 5-minutely to **maximum 1 mg**. **⚠ CONTRAINDICATED IN HYPERTENSION** — any hypertension, pre-eclampsia or eclampsia. An ergot alkaloid and potent vasoconstrictor: risks hypertensive crisis, stroke, myocardial ischaemia. Also contraindicated in severe cardiac/renal/hepatic/vascular disease, severe sepsis, and retained placenta. Causes marked vomiting — give an antiemetic. **The most examined contraindication in obstetrics**: PPH + BP 165/105 is testing whether you skip it.
4. **Misoprostol 800–1000 microgram SL or PR** — heat-stable, safe in hypertension and asthma, useful without refrigeration or IV access. Causes pyrexia (can exceed 40 °C), shivering, diarrhoea. Do not repeat within 2 h.
5. **Carboprost (15-methyl PGF2α) 250 microgram IM**, repeat every 15 min (up to 90 min intervals) to **maximum 2 mg = 8 doses**; 500 microgram intramyometrial off-label in theatre. **⚠ Avoid in asthma** (bronchospasm) and significant cardiac/pulmonary disease.
6. **Tranexamic acid 1 g IV over 10 min, as early as possible and within 3 h of onset** — mortality benefit falls off sharply after that (WOMAN trial). Repeat 1 g if bleeding continues after 30 min or recurs within 24 h. ⚑ TXA is given for **all** causes of PPH, not just suspected coagulopathy, and belongs early alongside the uterotonics, not as a rescue.
7. **Intrauterine balloon tamponade** (Bakri/condom catheter) — the next step if bimanual compression works, i.e. a positive "tamponade test". Fill ~300–500 mL, watch the drainage port for ongoing loss or blockage. Often avoids laparotomy.
8. **Laparotomy — conservative surgery**: **B-Lynch/compression sutures**, bilateral uterine artery ligation, internal iliac ligation; or **arterial embolisation** if stable and the service exists.
9. **Hysterectomy** — definitive. Decide early; delay is a recurring theme in maternal mortality reports. Subtotal unless the source is the lower segment/cervix (praevia/accreta).

**Transfusion.** 2 units RBC (group-specific or O-negative) if actively bleeding; in massive haemorrhage transfuse near-**1:1:1 (RBC:FFP:platelets), not below 2:1:1**. **Cryoprecipitate/fibrinogen concentrate when fibrinogen ≤2.5 g/L** — a far higher trigger than the trauma target of 1.5–2 g/L, because term fibrinogen is physiologically 4–6 g/L and a "normal-looking" 2.5 g/L already signals consumption. Keep platelets >50, temperature >36 °C, ionised calcium and pH corrected.

**The short version**
- Firm uterus + bleeding → trauma; boggy uterus + bleeding → tone.
- Ergometrine is banned in hypertension/pre-eclampsia; carboprost is the one to avoid in asthma.
- TXA 1 g IV early (<3 h) is standard for every PPH, not a rescue drug.
- Escalate mechanical → pharmacological → tamponade → compression sutures → hysterectomy, and do not delay the last step.

---

## 8.2 Retained placenta

Placenta undelivered **≥30 min after birth with active management** (≥60 min with physiological third stage). Incidence ~2–3%. It matters because it is both a *cause* of PPH (Tissue) and a *reason your uterotonics are failing*.

Empty the bladder (an IDC alone delivers a surprising number), re-attempt controlled cord traction, encourage skin-to-skin/nipple stimulation. **Oxytocin 10 IU IV/IM** may help; ⚑ intra-umbilical vein oxytocin is no longer recommended. **Do not give ergometrine** — tonic contraction entraps the placenta. If still undelivered → **manual removal in theatre under regional/general anaesthesia** with prophylactic antibiotics (endometritis risk).

**Suspect placenta accreta spectrum** if no cleavage plane can be found — clue is **previous caesarean(s) plus anterior placenta praevia**, or previous uterine surgery/curettage. Do not force separation: stop, call for help, prepare for haemorrhage/hysterectomy.

**The short version**
- 30 min (active) / 60 min (physiological) defines it.
- Empty the bladder, re-try CCT, oxytocin — never ergometrine.
- Previous LSCS + anterior praevia + non-separating placenta = accreta until proven otherwise.

---

## 8.3 Puerperal sepsis and secondary PPH

**Puerperal sepsis** = life-threatening organ dysfunction from a dysregulated response to a genital-tract or pregnancy-related infection in the puerperium. Historically the leading cause of maternal death and still a leading *direct* cause worldwide.

**Organisms.** *E. coli* is the most frequent isolate, but **Group A Streptococcus (S. pyogenes) causes the majority of sepsis deaths** — the killer to recognise. Also GBS, anaerobes, *Staph aureus*. GAS is often acquired from a household contact with pharyngitis — the stem may say so.

**Clue → source**

| Clue | Source |
|---|---|
| Tender bulky uterus, **offensive lochia**, subinvolution, fever after day 2 | Endometritis (commonest) |
| Fever + **wound erythema/discharge/dehiscence** post-LSCS or perineal repair | Wound infection; if pain out of proportion, crepitus, dusky skin → **necrotising fasciitis** |
| Fever + unilateral loin pain + dysuria | Pyelonephritis |
| Fever + **wedge-shaped tender red breast** | Mastitis (8.4) |
| Fever + calf/leg pain, or pleuritic chest pain/dyspnoea | VTE — remember PE mimics sepsis |
| Fever + **severe pain out of all proportion, diarrhoea/vomiting, rash, rapid collapse** | **Invasive GAS / toxic shock** |

**Red flags** (any two of): temp <36 or >38 °C, HR >110 sustained, RR >24 sustained, MAP <65, abnormal WCC, altered conscious state, poor perfusion. Beware: **maternal sepsis can present without pyrexia**, and physiological tachycardia masks early deterioration.

**Management.** Cultures (**two sets** blood cultures, plus HVS/wound/urine, throat swab if GAS suspected), **lactate**, FBC/U&E/LFT/coags, and **IV antibiotics within 60 min** — do not delay for cultures. Fluids, source control (evacuate retained products, drain collections, debride necrotic wound), early ICU involvement. **Empirical Australian regimen for unknown source postpartum: IV ampicillin + gentamicin + metronidazole. Add clindamycin if GAS or toxic shock is suspected** — it suppresses exotoxin production at the ribosome, and this is an exam favourite. IVIG in severe streptococcal toxic shock.

**Secondary PPH** (24 h – 6 weeks): **retained products of conception and endometritis**, usually together. Clue: bleeding restarts or worsens around day 7–14, offensive lochia, crampy pain, open os. FBC/CRP, cultures if febrile, **pelvic ultrasound** (clot and RPOC look similar early). Antibiotics ± uterotonics; **surgical evacuation only if bleeding is heavy or persistent**, after antibiotic cover — the puerperal uterus perforates easily and vigorous curettage risks Asherman syndrome. If bleeding persists, consider **gestational trophoblastic disease**: check βhCG and send tissue for histology.

---

## 8.4 Lactational mastitis and breast abscess

Affects roughly **1 in 5 Australian breastfeeding women in the first 6 months**, peaking at **weeks 2–3**. About **3–11% progress to abscess**.

**Clue → diagnosis.** A **unilateral, wedge-shaped (segmental) area of erythema, heat, firmness and tenderness with fever and flu-like myalgia** in a breastfeeding woman. Bilateral, diffuse, non-tender fullness in the first week is **engorgement**, not mastitis. A localised tender lump without systemic upset is a **blocked duct**.

**Organism:** *Staph aureus* predominates (MRSA in healthcare-associated cases); also streptococci and *S. epidermidis*.

**Management — the two examinable points**

1. **Flucloxacillin (or dicloxacillin) 500 mg PO qid for at least 5 days** is first-line in Australia.
